Useful Call-by-Value: Syntax and Semantics (Technical Report)
Pablo Barenbaum, Delia Kesner, Mariana Milicich

Abstract
. Useful evaluation, introduced by Accattoli and Dal Lago, is an optimised evaluation mechanism for functional programming languages. It relies on representing programs with sharing and imposing a restricted notion of useful substitutions , that intuitively disallows copying subterms that do not contribute to the progress of the computation. Initially defined in the framework of call-by-name, useful evaluation has since been extended to call-by-value (CBV), where it is shown that useful CBV is an optimisation of standard CBV that preserves its original semantics. This preservation result has been shown by means of syntactical rewriting techniques, requiring ad-hoc proofs. Consequently, the applicability of such optimisations to other models of computation remains limited. This work provides the first inductive definition of useful CBV evaluation. For that, we first restrict the substitution operation in the Value Substitution Calculus to be linear, yielding the lcbv ∘ \textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ} strategy. We then further restrict substitution in lcbv ∘ \textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ} , so that substitution contributes to the progress of the computation. This optimisation is the ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} strategy, and its notion of substitution is sensitive to the surrounding evaluation context, so it is non-trivial to capture it inductively. Moreover, we show that ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} is a sound and complete implementation of lcbv ∘ \textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ} , optimised to implement useful evaluation. As a further contribution, we show that an existing notion of usefulness in the literature, namely the GLAMoUr abstract machine, implements the ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} strategy with polynomial overhead in time. This establishes that ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} is time-invariant, i.e. , that the number of reduction steps to normal form in ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} can be used as a measure of time complexity. Defining ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} leads us to the first semantic model of useful CBV evaluation through system 𝒰 \mathcal{U} , a non-idempotent intersection type system. Our main result is a characterisation of termination for useful CBV evaluation via system 𝒰 \mathcal{U} : a term is typable in system 𝒰 \mathcal{U} if and only if it terminates in ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} . Additionally, system 𝒰 \mathcal{U} provides a quantitative interpretation for ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} , offering exact step-count information for program evaluation. Even though the specification of the operational semantics of ucbv ∙ \textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet} is highly complex, system 𝒰 \mathcal{U} is notably simple. As far as we know, system 𝒰 \mathcal{U} is one of the scarce quantitative type systems capturing exactly the substitution step-count for a call-by-value strategy.



1.
Introduction

The
\lambda
-calculus is the foundational model behind functional
programming languages and most proof assistants.
Implementing them
efficiently
requires designing an evaluation
mechanism that closely adheres to the operational semantics, while
optimising the key operations for resource efficiency, both for time
and space.
This creates a significant gap between the original model and its
optimised version, making it crucial to guarantee that both notions
have the same
observable behaviour
.

In the
\lambda
-calculus, evaluation strategies vary, with

call-by-name
(CBN) and
call-by-value
(CBV) being two
prominent examples.
In both cases, efficient implementations rely on term representations
with
sharing
.
For example, implementations typically use environments to bind a
variable to a shared subexpression, rather than textually substituting
the variable by many copies of the subexpression.
Formally,
\lambda
-terms with sharing can be represented with

explicit substitutions

(ESs)
1
1

1

See
(
Kesner, 2009
)
for a survey on ESs.
.
An ES binding a variable
x
to an expression
u
is written

[x\backslash u]
, and a term
t
affected by such ES is written

t[x\backslash u]
(which some authors note
“
\mathsf{let}\ x=u\ \mathsf{in}\ t
”).
The expression
t[x\backslash u]
means that all the free occurrences
of
x
in
t
are bound to
u
, and thus
u
is

shared
.
While the
\beta
-reduction rule of the
\lambda
-calculus is based
on the meta-level substitution operation
t\{x:=u\}
that
replaces all the free occurrences of
x
in
t
at once, some
calculi with ESs allow replacing a single occurrence of a variable,
such as
(x\,x)[x\backslash t]\to(t\,x)[x\backslash t]
.
This finer-grained substitution operation is called
linear substitution
.

Accattoli and Dal Lago
(
Accattoli and Lago, 2014
)
have proposed an optimised
evaluation mechanism, called
useful evaluation
, which
represents terms with ESs so that copy of shared subterms is
restricted to avoid
size explosion
.
This mechanism relies on two key
usefulness principles

—that we call
UP1
and
UP2
—.
Specifically, these two principles ensure that an occurrence of a
variable may be linearly substituted by an expression only if this
contributes to creating a
redex
2
2

2

Red
ucible
ex
pression.
.
Principle
UP1
(
Sharing Structures
) ensures that terms
headed by a free variable, or more precisely
structures
, such
as
x\,y
, always remain shared in ESs, and are never
copied.
Principle
UP2
(
Substituting Abstractions for Progress
)
ensures that an occurrence of a variable bound to a

\lambda
-abstraction may be substituted only if the variable is
applied to an argument.
See
Section

2
for more details.

Useful evaluation has been extended to

open CBV

(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2017
)
,
i.e.
, CBV over
terms that may contain free variables.
Unlike typical strategies that are defined by context closure rules
that are
local
in nature, useful evaluation is context-sensitive,
in the sense that steps involve side conditions of a
global
nature.
This dependency complicates inductive reasoning, as non-useful steps
can become useful when placed within specific contexts.
This makes it challenging to define useful evaluation by means of
inductive rules, as we discuss in the following sections.

Quantitative Interpretations.

The model of useful evaluation we define in this work is based on the

non-idempotent
flavour of
intersection types
(IT).
IT extend simple types with an intersection type constructor
\cap
,
allowing a program
t
to be typed with
\alpha\cap\beta
if

t
types with both
\alpha
and
\beta
independently.
Initially introduced as
models
to capture
qualitative

computational properties of functional programs
(
Coppo and Dezani-Ciancaglini, 1978
)
,
IT systems are able in particular to characterise termination of
evaluation strategies: a program
t
terminates in a given
evaluation strategy if and only if
t
is typable in an associated
type system.

In the original formulation of intersection types, the type
constructor
\cap
is considered to be
idempotent
, meaning
that
\sigma\cap\sigma=\sigma
.
So, an intersection
\alpha_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\alpha_{n}
can be
represented as the
set

\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\}
.
More recent works have adopted a
non-idempotent
intersection
type constructor
(
Gardner, 1994
;
Carvalho, 2007
)
.
Here, an intersection
\alpha_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\alpha_{n}
can be
represented as the
multiset

[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}]
.
Like their idempotent precursors, non-idempotent IT still allow
characterising operational properties of programs by means of
typability
(
Gardner, 1994
;
Carvalho, 2007
)
but also yield a
substantial improvement: they provide
quantitative
measures
about these properties.
For instance, from a typing derivation, it is possible to extract an

upper bound
—or even an
exact measure
— for the
number of steps which is necessary to reach the normal form.

More precisely, exact measures can be obtained in the so-called

tight
quantitative type systems
(
Accattoli et al., 2018
)
, by
decorating the type judgements with counters, and by imposing
a
tightness
condition on typing derivations to ensure that
they are
minimal
.
More specifically, typing judgements are enriched with integer

counters
specifying some quantitative information; for
instance, in a typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash^{m}t:\alpha
, the
counter
m
is used to capture the fact that
t
evaluates in
exactly
m
reduction steps to a normal form.

To (quantitatively) characterise termination for a given evaluation
strategy in such type systems, one relies on the results of

soundness
and
completeness
of the type system with
respect to the corresponding evaluation strategy.
Here,
quantitative
soundness means that for any
tight

type derivation of a program
t
with counter
m
, the program

t
evaluates to normal form in exactly
m
steps; this idea is
generalised for steps of many possible kinds with counters

m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}
.
Conversely, quantitative
completeness
means that every
reduction sequence to normal form of a given length corresponds to a

tight
typing derivation with appropriate counters.

Quantitative types
based on non-idempotent IT have been
applied to various evaluation strategies in the
\lambda
-calculus
for obtaining upper bounds and exact measures
(
Bernadet and Lengrand, 2013
;
Bucciarelli et al., 2017
;
Accattoli et al., 2018
;
de Carvalho, 2018
;
Kesner and Viso, 2022
)
,
as well as in the contexts of classical calculi
(
Kesner and Vial, 2017
;
Kesner and Vial, 2020
)
, call-by-value
(
Ehrhard, 2012
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2018
;
Guerrieri, 2018
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2022
)
,
call-by-need
(
Balabonski et al., 2017
;
Barenbaum et al., 2018
;
Accattoli et al., 2019
;
Leberle, 2021
)
,
call-by-push-value
(
Bucciarelli et al., 2020
)
, etc.
These type systems also serve as semantic interpretations, akin to

relational models
in the usual sense of linear logic
(
Girard, 1988
;
Bucciarelli and Ehrhard, 2001
)
.

Contributions.

This work is split in two parts.
The
first part
defines the first inductive specification of
useful evaluation, in particular in the framework of open CBV.
Although most functional programming languages are restricted to
closed terms (forbidding free variables), working in an open setting
(allowing free variables) broadens the applicability of our results.
Furthermore, being able to treat the open CBV case is a prerequisite
to deal in the future with other cases.
For example, Grégoire and Leroy study
strong
CBV evaluation
in
(
Grégoire and Leroy, 2002
)
, motivated by the implementation of proof
assistants based on dependent type theory.

In contrast to existing specifications of useful evaluation in the
literature, our calculus relies on
inductive
rules.
To define the new strategy
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, we proceed in two stages.
First, we recall in
Section

3
the

Value Substitution Calculus
(
vsc
)
(
Accattoli and Paolini, 2012
)
, which
is the starting point.
We then introduce the
linear
open CBV strategy (
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
),
which refines the
vsc
with
linear substitution
.
Moreover,
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
implements principle
UP1
but still not
UP2
.
Second, we introduce in
Section

4
the
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
strategy, by refining evaluation in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
to also implement principle

UP2
.
Our inductive approach to usefulness has been inspired by the
definition of strong call-by-need evaluation in
(
Balabonski et al., 2023
)
.
We also prove key operational properties of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
; in particular, it
enjoys the diamond property —and thus confluence.
In
Section

5
, we show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is indeed a
useful implementation of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
The relationship between the two strategies is established
syntactically, by means of rewriting techniques, and relying on a

partial unfolding
operation that performs
all
the
substitutions assigning values to variables.
The main result is that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
preserves the semantics of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
,
i.e.
,
if
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
reaches a result from a starting term then,
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reaches
the same result, up to partial unfolding.

Finally, in
Section

6
, we show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
can in turn be implemented by a lower-level abstract machine
implementing useful CBV, namely the GLAMoUr
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
)
.
This result not only connects
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
with a preexisting notion of usefulness,
but it also entails that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is
time-invariant
.
This means that the length of reductions to normal form can be taken
as a measure of time complexity.
More precisely,
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
can be simulated by a
reasonable
cost
model of computation in the sense of
(
Slot and van Emde Boas, 1984
)
(such as a
Turing machine or a RAM) with at most polynomial overhead in time.

The
second part
defines the first semantic model of useful
CBV evaluation, addressing the lack of a high-level, semantic
characterisation of this strategy.
In
Section

7
, we define a type system based on non-idempotent
intersection types called system
\mathcal{U}
, which is shown to
characterise termination in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
We show that system
\mathcal{U}
turns out to be the first semantic
model for useful CBV evaluation, and moreover, it provides
quantitative information on the evaluation strategy.
To do so, we equip type judgements of system
\mathcal{U}
with
counters, and we define a notion of
tightness
, intuitively
capturing minimality of derivations.
The counters are meant to capture the exact step-count for program
evaluation.
More precisely, we show that a term
t
is
tightly
typable
with counters
m
and
e
in system
\mathcal{U}
if and only if

t
terminates in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
in exactly
m
function application steps
and
e
substitution steps
(
Corollary

7.8
).

Our contribution is novel, as previous definitions of useful
evaluation (including useful CBV) lack semantic models and current
quantitative interpretations of CBV do not consider
usefulness
.
Moreover, except
(
Kesner and Viso, 2022
)
, entirely based
on the
persistent/consuming
paradigm, this is the only
quantitative type system for CBV that is able to count
substitution
steps
exactly
.

2.
Preliminary notions

In this section, we define the shared notions for both strategies

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
Next, we explain the two principles
UP1
and
UP2
that underlie
the notion of useful evaluation.
Moreover, we recall some background notions of rewriting theory.

Syntax.

Given a denumerable set of
variables
(
x,y,z,\ldots
),
the sets of
terms
(
t,u,s,\ldots
),

substitution contexts
(
\mathtt{L},\mathtt{L^{\prime}},\ldots
), and

values
(
v,w,\ldots
) are given by the following grammars:

t\mathrel{::=}x\mid\lambda x.\,t\mid t\,t\mid t[x\backslash t]\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{L}\mathrel{::=}\Diamond\mid\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\hskip 14.22636ptv\mathrel{::=}x\mid\lambda x.\,t

The set of terms includes
variables
,
abstractions
,

applications
, and
closures

t[x\backslash u]
representing
an
explicit substitution
(ES)
[x\backslash u]
on a term

t
.

Free
and
bound occurrences
of variables are defined as
usual, where free occurrences of
x
in
t
are bound in

t[x\backslash u]
.
We write
\mathsf{fv}(t)
and
\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{L})
for the set of free variables of a
term and of a context, respectively.
Terms are considered up to
\alpha
-renaming
of bound variables.
We write
t\mathtt{L}
for the
variable-capturing

replacement

of the hole
\Diamond
in
\mathtt{L}
by
t
, keeping the standard
notation
\mathtt{C}\langle t\rangle
for other kinds of contexts.
We write
t\in\mathsf{Abs}
if
t
is of the form
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}
and

t\{x:=u\}
stands for the
capture-avoiding

substitution
of the free occurrences of
x
with
u
in
t
.
The set of
reachable variables
of a term
t
is written

\mathsf{rv}(t)
and defined as:

\mathsf{rv}(x):=\{x\}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{rv}(\lambda x.\,t):=\varnothing\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{rv}(t\,u):=\mathsf{rv}(t)\cup\mathsf{rv}(u)\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{rv}(t[x\backslash u]):=(\mathsf{rv}(t)\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathsf{rv}(u)

Some recurring terms are the identity function
{\tt I}:=\lambda x.\,x

and the operator
\omega:=\lambda x.\,x\,x
.

Principles of Useful Evaluation.

As mentioned in the introduction, useful evaluation is motivated by
the two following key
usefulness principles
:

Sharing Structures
(
UP1
).
A term is a
structure
3
3

3

This name is borrowed
from
(
Balabonski et al., 2017
)
, but the terminology
inert term

is also used
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
)
.
if its
(full) unfolding

—the result of performing all the explicit substitutions by using
the capture-avoiding substitution— results in an application
headed by a variable,
i.e.
, of the form
x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{n}
,
where
(n\geq 0)
.
For example,

(x\,x)[x\backslash y\,{\tt I}][y\backslash z\,z]

is a structure, as it (fully) unfolds to

z\,z\,{\tt I}\,(z\,z\,{\tt I})
.
Structures must remain
shared
in useful evaluation, as
substituting a variable by a structure does not create a

function application redex
.
Thus, a term like

(x\,x)[x\backslash y\,{\tt I}][y\backslash z\,z]

is a normal form in useful evaluation.
The notion of structure just defined is context-independent.
Actually, we shall need a subtler
context-dependent
notion of
structure relative to a “structure frame” (defined in
Section

4
).
For instance
x\,y
should
not
be treated as a structure
under a context that binds
x
to an abstraction.

Substituting Abstractions for Progress
(
UP2
).
In useful evaluation, abstractions are substituted only if they
contribute to creating a function application redex,
thus ensuring progress in the computation.
For example, a reduction step like

x[x\backslash{\tt I}]\,y\to{\tt I}[x\backslash{\tt I}]\,y
is
useful, while the steps

x[x\backslash{\tt I}]\to{\tt I}[x\backslash{\tt I}]
and

(t\,x)[x\backslash{\tt I}]\to(t\,{\tt I})[x\backslash{\tt I}]

are not, as they do not contribute to creating function application redexes.
Some of these ideas can also be found in the literature on

optimal reduction
(see
e.g.
,
(
Yoshida, 1993
)
).

Background on Rewriting Theory.

We now introduce some general notions of reduction that will be used
all along the document.
Given a
reduction system

\mathcal{R}
, we denote by

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
the (one-step) reduction relation associated to
system
\mathcal{R}
.
We write
\rightarrow^{=}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}
for the
reflexive and reflexive-transitive closure of
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
,
and
\rightarrow^{n}_{\mathcal{R}}
for the composition of
n
-steps of

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
.
A term
t
is said to be
\mathcal{R}
-reducible
if there is

u
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
, and
t
is said
to be
\mathcal{R}
-irreducible
, or in
\mathcal{R}
-normal
form
, written
t\not\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}
, if there is no
u

such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}u
.
A term
t
is said to be
\mathcal{R}
-terminating
if there is
no infinite
\mathcal{R}
-sequence starting at
t
.
A term
t
is
\mathcal{R}
-diamond
(or enjoys the

\mathcal{R}
-diamond property) if
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{0}
and

t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{1}
with
t_{0}\neq t_{1}
imply there is

t^{\prime}
such that
t_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
and

t_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
.
A term
t
is
\mathcal{R}
-locally confluent

(resp.
\mathcal{R}
-confluent
) if
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{0}

and
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}t_{1}
(resp.
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}t_{0}

and
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}t_{1}
) imply there is
t^{\prime}
such that

t_{0}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
and
t_{1}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}t^{\prime}
.
A relation
\mathcal{R}
is
terminating
(resp.
diamond
,

locally confluent
,
confluent
) if and only if every term
is
\mathcal{R}
-terminating (resp.
\mathcal{R}
-diamond,

\mathcal{R}
-locally confluent,
\mathcal{R}
-confluent).
Any relation verifying the diamond property is in particular confluent,
and any relation verifying termination and local confluence is
confluent
(
Terese, 2003
)
.
Moreover, if
t
is confluent, then its
\mathcal{R}
-normal form,
if it exists, is unique
(
Terese, 2003
)
.

3.
Linear Call-by-Value

In this section, we define the
L
inear
CBV
strategy
(
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
), a first step towards useful evaluation.

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
fulfils principle
UP1
by keeping structures always shared,
but it does not fulfil
UP2
, as it allows substituting variables
by values unrestrictedly (see
Section

2
for the
definitions of
UP1
and
UP2
).

Furthermore,
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
adapts the substitution operation in the Value
Substitution Calculus (
vsc
) from
(
Accattoli and Paolini, 2012
)
to be

linear
by allowing the substitution of one occurrence of a
variable at a time.
We begin by explaining the difference between the (non-linear)
original substitution mechanism of the
vsc
, and the linear
substitution of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
We then formally define
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and conclude by stating its main
properties.

The Value Substitution Calculus.

Avoiding the substitution of variables by structures is a sufficient
mechanism to obtain an invariant implementation of open CBV (see
for instance
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2017
, Lemma 6)
).
One calculus implementing this mechanism for open terms is the
vsc
,
which consists of two kinds of reduction steps:

Distant beta
(
\mathsf{db}
) steps are given by the rule

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,u\to t[x\backslash u]\mathtt{L}
, where

\mathtt{L}
denotes a list of ES, called a
substitution context
.
A
\mathsf{db}
-step performs a
function application
by creating
an ES.
For example,

(\lambda x.\,{\tt I})\,(y\,y)\,z\to{\tt I}[x\backslash y\,y]\,z\to w[w\backslash z][x\backslash y\,y]
.
After the first step, the ES
[x\backslash y\,y]

appears to be blocking the interaction between
{\tt I}
and its argument

z
.
However, the
\mathsf{db}
-rule allows an arbitrary list of ESs to
appear between an abstraction and its argument, thus allowing to
fire the second reduction step in the example.

Substitution
steps (
\mathsf{sv}
) are given by the rule

t[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\to t\{x:=v\}\mathtt{L}
, which substitutes
a variable
x
by a value
v
,
pushing outside
the
substitution context
\mathtt{L}
originally accompanying the value.
For example,
(x\,x)[x\backslash\omega[y\backslash{\tt I}]]\to(\omega\,\omega)[y\backslash{\tt I}]
, where we recall that

\omega:=\lambda z.\,z\,z
.
The
vsc
implements principle
UP1
because it only allows
substituting variables by values (so that structures remain shared).
For example, the step

(x\,x)[x\backslash z\,z]\to z\,z\,(z\,z)
is not allowed.

Towards Linear Substitution.

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
refines the
vsc
by implementing
linear substitution
,
which proceeds by
micro-steps
, replacing one occurrence of a
variable at a time.
Linear substitution is a prerequisite —but not sufficient— to
fulfil the principle
UP2
,
i.e.
, to
fully
implement useful
evaluation.
Specifically, when a variable occurs multiple times in a term,
substituting it with an abstraction may create a function application
in some cases but not in others. For example, in the step

(z\,(\underline{x}\,y)\,\overline{x})[x\backslash{\tt I}]\to z\,({\tt I}\,y)\,{\tt I}
,
substituting the underlined occurrence of
x
by
{\tt I}
creates
a
\mathsf{db}
-redex (and thus it is useful) while substituting the
overlined occurrence of
x
by
{\tt I}
does not contribute to
creating a
\mathsf{db}
-redex.

The Linear CBV Calculus.

We now define the Linear CBV strategy
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
, which refines the
vsc
by implementing linear substitution.
We adopt a formulation of rules based on the style of
(
Balabonski et al., 2023
)
,
which we also follow to formulate our inductive notion of useful evaluation,

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, in
Section

4
.

Formally, we define a family of binary relations
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}
,
where
\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv},\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}\}

distinguishes the
step kind
, with
x
being a variable and

v
a value such that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(v)
.
The set of
free variables
of a step kind
\rho
is given
by
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{db}):=\varnothing
,
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{lsv}):=\varnothing
, and

\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}):=\{x\}\cup\mathsf{fv}(v)
.
The
linear
relation
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}
of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
is defined
inductively as follows:

\frac{}{(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}t[x\backslash u]\mathtt{L}}\textup{{db${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}v}\textup{{sub${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}t^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}}\textup{{lsv${}^{\circ}$}}

\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}}{t\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}\,u}\textup{{appL${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}u^{\prime}}{t\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t\,u^{\prime}}\textup{{appR${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}\textup{{esL${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}u^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash u^{\prime}]}\textup{{esR${}^{\circ}$}}

Rules
db
∘
,
sub
∘
, and
lsv
∘
define the three kinds of
reduction steps, whereas
appL
∘
,
appR
∘
,
esL
∘
, and

esR
∘
are congruence rules, and propagate any step kind.
Note that evaluation is
weak
and does not proceed within abstractions.
A step of the form
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}u
represents a

distant beta
step.
The reduction steps
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}u
and

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}u
constitute two kinds of
substitution
steps.
In the first kind,
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}u
,
one

free occurrence of
x
in
t
is substituted with
v
.
In the second kind,
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}u
,
one
bound
occurrence of
x
is substituted with
v
, provided
x
is
bound to a term of the form
v\mathtt{L}
by an ES;
lsv
∘
is the
only rule that introduces an
\mathsf{lsv}
-step.
Moreover, each step substituting a bound variable (
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}
)
relies internally on a step that substitutes a free variable
(
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}
).
These substitutions steps focus on a
single
variable
occurrence, thus we say the substitution operation is
linear
.
One noteworthy remark is that rule
sub
∘
allows substitution of a
variable for
any
value, thus the reduction steps

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{1})}}v_{1}
and

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{2})}}v_{2}
are valid, so
confluence

of
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(\_,\_)}}
makes no sense: these steps are only an
auxiliary mechanism to define
\mathsf{lsv}
-steps, and in particular to
define the notion of
linear
substitution of a single occurrence
of a variable by a value.
At the end of this section, we prove the property of confluence on
the
top-level

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
reduction, defined as

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathtt{top}}:=\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}\cup\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}
.
Moreover, the relation
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}
is shown to be terminating
(
Corollary

C.15
).

Along with rule
db
∘
, an application can be evaluated using rules

appL
∘
and
appR
∘
, which reduce within the left and right subterms,
respectively.
Since rules
db
∘
,
appL
∘
, and
appR
∘
overlap, reduction
is
non-deterministic
.
For example:

x[x\backslash{\tt I}]\,({\tt I}\,{\tt I})\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}{\tt I}\,{\tt I}\,({\tt I}\,{\tt I})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}{\tt I}\,{\tt I}\,x[x\backslash{\tt I}]

The congruence rules
esL
∘
and
esR
∘
allow reduction on the
left side of a term and within the argument of an ES, respectively.
These rules overlap as well.
A key restriction in rule
esL
∘
is that the variable
x
bound
by the ES
[x\backslash u]
must not occur free in the step kind
\rho
.
This restriction is to avoid variable capture; for example, it ensures
that a “pathological” step like

y[x\backslash z]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,x)}}x[x\backslash z]

cannot be derived from the valid step

y\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,x)}}x
.

Example 3.1.

The following is a reduction sequence to normal form in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}&(\lambda x.\,z\,x\,(x\,y))\,{\tt I}&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}&(z\,x\,(x\,y))[x\backslash{\tt I}]\\
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}&(z\,{\tt I}\,(x\,y))[x\backslash{\tt I}]&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}&(z\,{\tt I}\,({\tt I}\,y))[x\backslash{\tt I}]\\
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}&(z\,{\tt I}\,(w[w\backslash y]))[x\backslash{\tt I}]&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}&(z\,{\tt I}\,(y[w\backslash y]))[x\backslash{\tt I}]\end{array}

Confluence

Despite the overlaps of some reduction rules, it is straightforward
to show that top-level
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}
reduction is confluent:

Proposition 3.2.

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathtt{top}}
is confluent.

4.
Useful Call-by-Value

In this section, we refine the notion of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
reduction introduced in

Section

3
, the resulting strategy is called
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
While preserving principle
UP1
, already present in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
,
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
evaluation also implements principle
UP2
, which is to be captured

inductively
.
To achieve useful CBV evaluation, we define a family of reduction
relations indexed by certain
parameters
representing the
essential information from the surrounding evaluation contexts.
This is the minimal data necessary to decide whether a substitution
step is useful or not.

After defining the new strategy
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, we give an inductive
characterisation of its normal forms (
Theorem

4.4
).
Following this result, we show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
satisfies the Diamond
Property (
Theorem

4.5
), which ensures that
the length of a reduction sequence to normal form does not depend on
the particular order of redexes chosen to reduce terms.

Towards the Useful CBV Strategy.

To implement principle
UP2
in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
it is necessary to ensure
that substitution contributes to the
progress
of the evaluation.
For example, the substitution step

(x\,t)[x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]\to(y\,t)[x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]

is (indirectly) useful because the occurrence of
x
is applied to
an argument
t
, and substituting
x
by
y
then
contributes to creating the underlined redex

(\underline{{\tt I}\,t})[x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]
after one
more substitution step.
This motivates the need to identify
hereditary abstractions
,
which intuitively include abstractions and variables hereditarily bound to abstractions,
such as
y
in the example.
Formally, let
\mathcal{A}
be a set of variables, called an
abstraction frame
.
The set of
hereditary abstractions
under
\mathcal{A}
is
written
\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
and defined below:

\frac{}{\lambda x.\,t\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}\textup{{h-lam}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{x\!\in\!\mathcal{A}}{x\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}\textup{{h-var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\!\notin\!\mathcal{A}}{t[x\backslash u]\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}\textup{{h-sub${}_{1}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\!\notin\!\mathcal{A}\hskip 8.5359ptu\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t[x\backslash u]\!\in\!\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}\textup{{h-sub${}_{2}$}}

The abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
keeps track of variables that are bound
to hereditary abstractions.
Every abstraction is a hereditary abstraction (by the first rule),
while a variable is a hereditary abstraction if it appears in the abstraction frame
(by the second rule).
In the case of terms with ESs, the crucial point is that the bound variable
x
may be
added to the abstraction frame only if it is bound to a hereditary abstraction
(third and fourth rule).
For example,
x[x\backslash\lambda y.\,y]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
for
any
\mathcal{A}
, and

x[z\backslash w][x\backslash y]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
if and
only if
y\in\mathcal{A}
.
Note also that applications are never hereditary abstractions.

On the other hand, it is necessary to identify
structures
,
which intuitively include both applied variables and variables
hereditarily bound to structures.
Formally, let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of variables, called a
structure frame
.
The set of
structures under
\mathcal{S}
is written
\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}

and defined below:

\frac{x\!\in\!\mathcal{S}}{x\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{s-var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{t\,u\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{s-app}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\!\notin\!\mathcal{S}}{t[x\backslash u]\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{s-sub${}_{1}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\!\notin\!\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptu\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{t[x\backslash u]\!\in\!\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{s-sub${}_{2}$}}

The structure frame
\mathcal{S}
keeps track of variables that are bound
to structures.
Note that no abstraction belongs to the set of structures, for any
\mathcal{S}
.
An application is a structure if its left subterm is itself a structure,
thus excluding
\mathsf{db}
-redexes from the set of structures under any
\mathcal{S}
.
Variables in the structure frame are structures as well.
For terms with ESs, the intuitions behind the third and fourth rules
are analogous to the corresponding ones for the predicate
\mathsf{HA}
, respectively.
For example, performing the substitution on the left subterm in

x[x\backslash y_{1}\,y_{2}]\,z
does not create
any
\mathsf{db}
-redex, so
x[x\backslash y_{1}\,y_{2}]
is
considered a structure.
Similarly,
(x\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]
is a structure, even if

y
is bound to an abstraction.

We summarise below some key (but easy) properties of hereditary
abstractions and structures:

Remark 4.1.

(1) If
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
;
(2) A term of the form
t\,u
is never in
\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
;
(3) If
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
t=v\mathtt{L}
;
(4) If
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
;
(5) A term of the form
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}
is never in
\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
;
(6) For any
\mathtt{L}
, one has that
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

The Useful CBV Reduction.

We can now formally define the strategy
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
specified as a family
of binary relations
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, where

\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv},\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}\}
is a step
kind,
\mathcal{A}
is an abstraction frame,
\mathcal{S}
is a structure frame,
and
\mu\in\{@,{\text{$\not$\!@}}\}
is a positional flag.
The
reduction relation

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
of

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is inductively defined as follows:

\frac{}{(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t[x\backslash u]\mathtt{L}}\textup{{db${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v}\textup{{sub${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptv\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}}\textup{{lsv${}^{\bullet}$}}

\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}t^{\prime}}{t\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}\,u}\textup{{appL${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}u^{\prime}}{t\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t\,u^{\prime}}\textup{{appR${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}u^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t[x\backslash u^{\prime}]}\textup{{esR${}^{\bullet}$}}

\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}\textup{{esLA${}^{\bullet}$}}

\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}\textup{{esLS${}^{\bullet}$}}

Note that each
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
step is also a
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
step,
i.e.
,

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}\ \subseteq\ \xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}
.
Also, the reduction relation defined above is
non-erasing
: if

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
with

\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}
, then
\mathsf{fv}(t)=\mathsf{fv}(t^{\prime})
.
As in the previous section, rules
db
∙
,
sub
∙
, and
lsv
∙
introduce the three possible step kinds, while all other cases
(
appL
∙
,
appR
∙
,
esLA
∙
,
esLS
∙
, and
esR
∙
)
are congruence rules for steps of an arbitrary step kind
\rho
.
Reduction is
weak
since there are no rules to evaluate under
abstractions.

Rule
db
∙
performs a function application step, identical to rule

db
∘
in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
Steps of the form
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u

and
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u
represent two different
kinds of
substitution steps
, as in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
: the former substitutes

one
free occurrence of a variable, while the latter substitutes

one
bound occurrence.
Only rule
lsv
∙
creates an
\mathsf{lsv}
-step.
Each application of rule
lsv
∙
relies internally on a

\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}
-step, where
v
must be a hereditary abstraction,
a restriction not required in rule
lsv
∘
rule in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
, but that
it is crucial to implement principle
UP2
(
substituting abstractions for progress
).

Substituting a free variable in a term
t
using rule
sub
∙
is
only possible when
t
is in an applied position because this
ensures the progress of the computation.
The following example shows an instance of the
sub
∙
rule:

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.61458pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,{\tt I})},\{x\},\{z\},@}{\tt I}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right = {{sub${}^{\bullet}$}} }{\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt{\tt I}[y\backslash z]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\varnothing}}}{x[x\backslash{\tt I}[y\backslash z]]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.61458pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\{z\},@}{\tt I}[x\backslash{\tt I}][y\backslash z]}\quad\textsc{Right = {{lsv${}^{\bullet}$}} }}{x[x\backslash{\tt I}[y\backslash z]]\,w\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\{z\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}{\tt I}[x\backslash{\tt I}][y\backslash z]w}\textup{{appL${}^{\bullet}$}}

The congruence rules
appL
∙
and
appR
∙
perform reduction on the
application constructor.
Rule
appR
∙
additionally requires the left subterm of the application
to be a structure, avoiding a possible overlap with rule
db
∙
.
Still, rules
appL
∙
and
appR
∙
overlap, so reduction is
non-deterministic, like in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
For example:

x\,y[y\backslash{\tt I}]\,({\tt I}\,{\tt I})\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}x\,({\tt I}\,{\tt I})\,({\tt I}\,{\tt I})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}x\,({\tt I}\,{\tt I})\,y[y\backslash{\tt I}]

The congruence rules
esR
∙
,
esLA
∙
, and
esLS
∙
apply
to closures.
Rule
esR
∙
allows reducing the argument of any ESs, while rules

esLA
∙
and
esLS
∙
allow reduction of the left side of
the closure under specific conditions:

esLA
∙
(resp.
esLS
∙
) applies only if the substitution
argument is a hereditary abstraction (resp. a structure).
Note that rules
esLA
∙
and
esLS
∙
force reducing the
argument
u
of a closure
t[x\backslash u]
until it becomes
“stuck”, after which evaluation can proceed to the body
t
.
This behaviour aligns with a CBV strategy, covering all cases in
terminating terms, as every terminating term is eventually either a
hereditary abstraction or a structure
(
cf.

Lemma

B.4
in
Appendix

B
).
These rules also enforce that the variable
x
bound by the ES

[x\backslash u]
does not occur free in the step kind
\rho
,
as in rule
esL
∘
in
Section

3
.
This restriction prevents variable capture in steps like

y[x\backslash{\tt I}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,x)},\{y\},\varnothing,@}x[x\backslash{\tt I}]
.
Note that there is a possible overlap between rule

esR
∙
and rules
esLA
∙
, and
esLS
∙
.

A term
t
is
(\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu)
-reducible

if there exists a term
t^{\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
A term
t
belongs to the set
\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
if

t
is not
(\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu)
-reducible.

Up to this point, hereditary abstractions and structures show distinct
and disjoint behaviours within
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, which are put in evidence
through the distinction between abstraction frames and structure frames.
An abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
and a structure frame
\mathcal{S}
are said
to verify the
correctness property
for
t
, written

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
, if
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}=\varnothing
and

\mathsf{fv}(t)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
.
This property is implicitly assumed in theorems and lemmas,
i.e.
,
whenever we write
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, we always
keep
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
as an
invariant
.
Note that
\mathsf{inv}{(\varnothing,\mathsf{fv}(t),t)}
always holds, so for a top-level
term
t
, we may take
\mathcal{A}:=\varnothing
and
\mathcal{S}:=\mathsf{fv}(t)
.

Example 4.2.

The following is an
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction sequence to normal form:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}&(\lambda x.\,z\,x\,(x\,y))\,{\tt I}&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\{z,y\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}&(z\,x\,(x\,y))[x\backslash{\tt I}]\\
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\{z,y\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}&(z\,x\,({\tt I}\,y))[x\backslash{\tt I}]&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\{z,y\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}&(z\,x\,(w[w\backslash y]))[x\backslash{\tt I}]\end{array}

Compare this reduction sequence with the corresponding evaluation of
the same term in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
(
Example

3.1
).
In
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, the leftmost occurrence of
x
and the occurrence of

w
are not substituted, because these substitutions would not
satisfy principle
UP2
: they do not contribute to creating
a
\mathsf{db}
-redex.

Example 4.3.

The following is another example of a
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction sequence to normal form:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}&(x\,z)[x\backslash y[y\backslash{\tt I}]]&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\{z\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}&(y\,z)[x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]\\
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\{z\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}&({\tt I}\,z)[x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]&\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\{z\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}&x_{1}[x_{1}\backslash z][x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]\end{array}

As discussed in
Section

1
, useful evaluation is

context-sensitive
, as the context surrounding a term
determines whether a step is useful or not.
For instance, the term
t=x[x\backslash{\tt I}]
is already in normal
form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, as substituting
x
by
{\tt I}
is not useful,
because it does not create a
\mathsf{db}
-redex.
However, if
t
is located to the left of an application, such as
in
x[x\backslash{\tt I}]\,(y\,{\tt I})
, the substitution of
x

by
{\tt I}
becomes useful, and yields the following reduction sequence:

\hfill x[x\backslash{\tt I}]\,(y\,{\tt I})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\{y\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}{\tt I}[x\backslash{\tt I}]\,(y\,{\tt I})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\{y\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}z[z\backslash y\,{\tt I}][x\backslash{\tt I}]\hfill

Conversely, a useful step like

(x\,u)[x\backslash{\tt I}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}({\tt I}\,u)[x\backslash{\tt I}]
may
not be allowed if the term is located below a context such as

\lambda y.\,\Diamond
; in fact, note that

\lambda y.\,(x\,u)[x\backslash{\tt I}]
cannot be reduced.

Operational Properties.

We state two theorems about
the operational properties of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.

Theorem

4.4
provides an inductive
characterisation of the set of normal forms, while

Theorem

4.5
shows that it enjoys a very
strong form of confluence, namely the diamond property.
See
Appendix

B
for more details and complete proofs.

Theorem 4.4 (Characterisation of normal forms).

The set of irreducible terms
\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

is exactly the set
\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
defined inductively as below:

\frac{x\in\mathcal{A}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}}{x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{\lambda x.\,t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-lam${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}{t\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-app${}^{\bullet}$}}

\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esA${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esS${}^{\bullet}$}}

Theorem 4.5 (Diamond Property).

Let
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\varnothing,\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}t_{1}

and
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\varnothing,\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}t_{2}
,
where
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
and

\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}

and
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{fv}(t)
.
Then, there exists
t^{\prime}
such that

t_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\varnothing,\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}t^{\prime}
and

t_{2}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\varnothing,\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}t^{\prime}
.

The two announced consequences of the previous result follow:

Corollary 4.6.

Any two reduction sequences to normal form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
have the same
number of
\mathsf{db}
and
\mathsf{lsv}
steps.

As with
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
, the
confluence
of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
holds only for the

top-level
step kinds
\mathsf{db}
and
\mathsf{lsv}
.
Let
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}{:=}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\varnothing,\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\cup\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\varnothing,\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}

denote the top-level
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction, then:

Corollary 4.7.

The reduction
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}
is confluent.

5.
Relating Linear and Useful Call-by-Value

This section aims to establish a connection between
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
,
defined in
Sections

3
and
4
respectively.
We show that usefulness in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is a (complete) restriction of

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
:
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
evaluates to equivalent normal forms as in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
while omitting certain substitution steps, specifically those that do
not contribute to the creation of
\mathsf{db}
-redexes.

Simulating
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
with
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is straightforward, as each
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
step
is also a
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
step, as we mention in
Section

4
.
However, relating both strategies in the opposite direction is much
more delicate, since not all
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
steps are useful:
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
disallows
some substitution steps that
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
allows.
For example, the step

t=(x\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}(x\,{\tt I})[y\backslash{\tt I}]=u
is not useful.
In fact,
t
is a normal form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
(details in

Proposition

5.1
), whereas in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
,

t
is a redex whose normal form is
u
.

Although normal forms differ between the two formalisms, they are

structurally equivalent
, as
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
evaluates terms further than

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
To relate them precisely, we restrict the standard notion of unfolding
to a subtler one.
Recall that in calculi with ESs, the (full) unfolding of a term is an
operation that performs
all
substitutions.
For example, the (full) unfolding of

x[x\backslash y\,y][y\backslash z\,z]
is

z\,z\,(z\,z)[x\backslash y\,y][y\backslash z\,z]
.
In this section, however, the notion unfolding must be defined in a
controlled way.
For instance, unfolding the term
x[x\backslash{\tt I}]
, which is a
normal form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, yields its corresponding normal form

{\tt I}[y\backslash{\tt I}]
in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
Yet the new unfolding operation does not need to unfold
all

ESs: for example, in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
,
(\lambda x.\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]
is

not
unfolded to
(\lambda x.\,{\tt I})[y\backslash{\tt I}]
(due to weak evaluation),
and
x[x\backslash y\,y]
is not unfolded to

(y\,y)[x\backslash y\,y]
(since variables are
never substituted by structures).
Intuitively, the new notion of unfolding selectively performs only
those substitutions on
reachable
variables bound to values,
thus we name it
partial unfolding
.

The remainder of this section is organised as follows.
We first define the partial unfolding of a term with respect to a
value assignment.
We then relate
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
via two technical results
(
Proposition

5.1
), showing that (1) the
partial unfolding of normal forms in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
always yields a normal
form in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
, and (2) redexes in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
remain reducible in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.

Partial Unfolding

To formalise our approach, we define a
value assignment
,
written
\sigma
, as a partial function that maps variables to values.
The
domain
and
image
of a value assignment
\sigma

are denoted by
\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
and
\mathsf{im}(\sigma)
, respectively.
To ensure idempotence, we require that
\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
and the free
variables in
\mathsf{im}(\sigma)
to be disjoint,
i.e.
,

\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{im}(\sigma))=\varnothing
.
The empty value assignment is denoted with
\cdot
.

We now define the
partial unfolding
of a term
t
under a
value assignment
\sigma
, written
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
, as follows:

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l}{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma(x)&\text{if }x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\\
x&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
{(\lambda x.\,t)}^{\downarrow\sigma}&:=&\lambda x.\,t\\
{(t\,u)}^{\downarrow\sigma}&:=&{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\\
{t[x\backslash u]}^{\downarrow\sigma}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{t}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}&\text{if }{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L},x\in\mathsf{rv}(t)\\
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}]&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

This definition has two base cases.
First, the partial unfolding causes no effect on abstractions,
reflecting the fact that evaluation is weak.
Second, when partially unfolding a variable
x
, if

x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, the definition mimics a substitution step of
the form
x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,\sigma(x))}}v
; otherwise,

x
remains unchanged, meaning it will not be substituted by a value.

Expanding
\sigma
with
x\mapsto v
still produces a value
assignment.
By
\alpha
-conversion, we assume
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
,

x\notin\mathsf{fv}(v)
, and for any
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{im}(\sigma)
,

x\notin\mathsf{fv}(v^{\prime})
.
Partial unfolding at top-level is always performed under the empty
value assignment.
Some examples of such partial unfoldings follow:

{(\lambda z.\,x)[x\backslash y]}^{\downarrow}=(\lambda z.\,x)[x\backslash y]\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt{(x\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]}^{\downarrow}=(x\,{\tt I})[y\backslash{\tt I}]\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt{x[x\backslash y[z\backslash{\tt I}]]}^{\downarrow}=y[x\backslash y][z\backslash{\tt I}]

In some cases, the left subterm of a closure is partially unfolded
under the original value assignment, as in the first example,
where
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(\lambda z.\,x)
.
However, in other cases, the value assignment must be extended.
In the last example, partially unfolding the subterm
x
extends
the value assignment to
(x\mapsto y)
.

At top-level, the partial unfolding of any term
t
is the (unique)

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}
normal form of
t
; see
Section

C.1

for details.

Relating Reduction Steps and Normal Forms

We now formally state the existing relation between
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
Our goal is to show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
simulates
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
while reaching
equivalent normal forms, up to partial unfolding.
To do this, we state the following result, consisting of two parts:
(1) the partial unfolding of a useful normal form is a linear normal form; (2) the partial unfolding of a reducible term in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is either a
\mathsf{db}
-redex in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
or an abstraction in an applied
position.
This second condition justifies the name “useful” in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
because
it implies that any substitution step must contribute to the creation
of a
\mathsf{db}
-redex (see
Section

1
).

Recall that
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathtt{top}}
and
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}
denote the top-level

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction, respectively.
The following proposition links
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction at the
top-level case:

Proposition 5.1.

Let
t
be a term and
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{fv}(t)
.
Then:

1.

If
t
is
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}
-irreducible,
then
{t}^{\downarrow}
is
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathtt{top}}
-irreducible.

2.

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}t^{\prime}
, then there exists
t^{\prime\prime}

such that
{t}^{\downarrow}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}t^{\prime\prime}
.

This statement is generalised for arbitrary parameters
(
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
,
\mu
, etc.) in the appendix
(
Proposition

C.25
), which has some subtleties.

To illustrate statement
1
, let
us take the term
(x\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]
, which is

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\{x\}}
-irreducible.
Its partial unfolding is
(x\,{\tt I})[y\backslash{\tt I}]
, which is

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathtt{top}}
-irreducible.
As an example of statement

2
,
consider the
\mathsf{lsv}
-step

t=(x\,y)[x\backslash{\tt I}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\{y\}}({\tt I}\,y)[x\backslash{\tt I}]=t^{\prime}={t}^{\downarrow}
;
note that
t^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}x_{1}[x_{1}\backslash x][y\backslash{\tt I}]
.

From these propositions, we conclude:

Corollary 5.2.

A term
t
is
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathsf{fv}(t)}
-irreducible if and only if

{t}^{\downarrow}
is
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathtt{top}}
-irreducible.

6.
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is Invariant

In this section, we relate our
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
strategy to the GLAMoUr abstract machine introduced in
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
)
, which is an
existing implementation of usefulness.
As a consequence, we yield that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is
time-invariant
.
That is, the number of reduction steps to normal form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
can
be used as a measure of time complexity.

We proceed in two stages, following
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
)
.
On one hand, we prove a
high-level implementation
theorem
(
Theorem

6.3
),
stating that reduction in the fireball calculus
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
)

can be simulated by reduction in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, with
quadratic

overhead in time.
On the other hand, we prove a
low-level implementation
theorem
(
Theorem

6.4
),
stating that reduction in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
can be implemented by the GLAMoUr abstract machine, with
linear
overhead in time.
By composing these results, we obtain that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is an invariant
implementation of open CBV (
Corollary

6.5
).
The key property in this section is a simulation result
(
Lemma

6.1
), which embeds the GLAMoUr abstract machine
into
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.

We state the main results, while most of the technical details are
in
Appendix

D
.
The definitions and proofs in this section follow well-known
methodologies (
e.g.
,
(
Accattoli et al., 2014
;
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2017
)
).

Stability Notions

Given an abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
and a structure frame
\mathcal{S}
, a
term
t
is said to be
rigid
under
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
if

t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
or
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
In
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, evaluating a closure
t[x\backslash u]
proceeds in the
body
t
only when the argument
u
is rigid.
To better align with low-level abstract machines, we define for any
abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
and structure frame
\mathcal{S}
the set of

stable terms
under
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
as those in which every ES
argument is rigid under
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.
We introduce a reduction relation we dub
stable reduction
and
is written
{\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}
, such that

{\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\subseteq{\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}
.
Sometimes, we write
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}
if the parameters are clear from the context.
Stable reduction forces arguments of applications to be rigid reduction proceeds.
Therefore, in this setting, rule
appL
∙
allows to reduce the head

t
of an application
t\,u
only if
u
is rigid,
and rule
db
∙
allows to contract a
\mathsf{db}
-redex only if its
argument is rigid. Stable reduction preserves stable terms and enjoys
the diamond property.

Embedding the GLAMoUr into useful Open CBV

We start by briefly recalling the syntax of the GLAMoUr.
Full details on the GLAMoUr transitions and the remaining definitions
can be found in
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2017
)
and
Appendix

D
.

The syntax of the GLAMoUr is given by the following
grammar, defining

states

(s,s^{\prime},\ldots)
,

dumps

(D,D^{\prime},\ldots)
,

stacks

(\pi,\pi^{\prime},\ldots)
,

stack items

(\phi,\psi,\ldots)
, and

global environments

(E,E^{\prime},\ldots)
:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}s&\mathrel{::=}&(D,\mathbf{t},\pi,E)&&\phi&\mathrel{::=}&\mathbf{t}\mid(\mathbf{t},\pi)\\
D&\mathrel{::=}&\epsilon\mid D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&&E&\mathrel{::=}&\epsilon\mid[x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E\\
\pi&\mathrel{::=}&\epsilon\mid\phi^{l}:\pi\end{array}

where
l\in\{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{S}\}
are
labels
decorating stack items.
Here,
codes

(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u},\ldots)
are terms with no ESs,
but they are
not
considered up to
\alpha
-equivalence.
By convention, labels indicate the shape of
\phi
.
In particular,
l=\mathbb{A}
if and only if
\phi
is of the
form
\mathbf{t}
, and
l=\mathbb{S}
if and only if
\phi
is of the
form
(\mathbf{t},\pi)
.
Intuitively, these labels indicate whether a stack item unfolds to
a hereditary abstraction (
\mathbb{A}
) or a structure (
\mathbb{S}
).
In a state
s=(D,\mathbf{t},\pi,E)
,
the code
\mathbf{t}
is called the
focus
of
s
.
A state
s_{0}
is
initial
if it is of the form

s_{0}=(\epsilon,\mathbf{t},\epsilon,\epsilon)
.

The decode function
\{\!\!\{\_\}\!\!\}
operates over the syntactic elements
of the GLAMoUr and returns terms in the syntax of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
The function is defined recursively as:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}\{\!\!\{(D,\mathbf{t},\pi,E)\}\!\!\}&:=&\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}\langle\{\!\!\{D\}\!\!\}\langle\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\rangle\rangle\rangle&&\{\!\!\{[x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E\}\!\!\}&:=&\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}\langle\Diamond[x\backslash\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}]\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{\epsilon\}\!\!\}&:=&\Diamond&&\{\!\!\{(\mathbf{t},\pi)^{l}\}\!\!\}&:=&\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)\}\!\!\}&:=&\{\!\!\{D\}\!\!\}\langle\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\Diamond\rangle\rangle&&\{\!\!\{\mathbf{t}^{l}\}\!\!\}&:=&t\\
\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}:\pi\}\!\!\}&:=&\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle\Diamond\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}\rangle&&\{\!\!\{\mathbf{t}\}\!\!\}&:=&t\end{array}

In the two last lines on the right column,
t
denotes a term which
is
\alpha
-equivalent to
\mathbf{t}
.

As is typical when relating abstract machines and calculi with ESs
(see
e.g.
,
(
Accattoli et al., 2014
)
), the GLAMoUr abstract machine can be
simulated in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
up to
structural equivalence

of terms
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2016
)
, denoted
\equiv
,
defined as the least equivalence relation closed by weak contexts
(
i.e.
, contexts that do not enter inside abstractions) of the four following
axioms:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\HS}rcl}t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]&\equiv&t[y\backslash s][x\backslash u]\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]&\equiv&t[x\backslash u[y\backslash s]]\\
(t\,u)[x\backslash s]&\equiv&t[x\backslash s]\,u\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&(t\,u)[x\backslash s]&\equiv&t\,u[x\backslash s]\end{array}

All these axioms assume that variable-capture is avoided,
for example, the first equation assumes that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)

and
y\notin\mathsf{fv}(u)
.

There are seven kinds of transitions in the GLAMoUr:
transitions (
s\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{m}}}s^{\prime}
) for function applications,
transitions (
s\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{e}}}s^{\prime}
) for linear substitutions,
and
administrative
transitions

s\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{i}}s^{\prime}
with
i\in\{1..5\}
,
for changing the evaluation focus without computing.
Formally, the transitions of the GLAMoUr abstract machine are:

{\small\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|ccc|c|c|ccc}D&\mathbf{t}\,\mathbf{s}&\pi&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{1}}&D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{s}&\epsilon&E\\
D&\mathbf{\lambda x.\,t}&\phi^{l}:\pi&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{m}}}&D&\mathbf{t}&\pi&[x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{\lambda x.\,s}&\epsilon&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{2}}&D&\mathbf{t}&(\mathbf{\lambda x.\,s})^{\mathbb{A}}:\pi&E\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{x}&\pi^{\prime}&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{3}}&D&\mathbf{t}&(\mathbf{x},\pi^{\prime})^{\mathbb{S}}:\pi&E\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{x}&\pi^{\prime}&E_{1}:[x\backslash\phi^{\mathbb{S}}]:E_{2}&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{4}}&D&\mathbf{t}&(\mathbf{x},\pi^{\prime})^{\mathbb{S}}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\phi^{\mathbb{S}}]:E_{2}\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{x}&\epsilon&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{5}}&D&\mathbf{t}&\mathbf{x}^{\mathbb{A}}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}\\
D&\mathbf{x}&\phi^{l}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{e}}}&D&\mathbf{s}^{\alpha}&\phi^{l}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}\end{array}}

where
\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}
is any code
\alpha
-equivalent to

\mathbf{u}
that preserves well-naming of the machine.
Moreover, in the transition
\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{3}}
, the variable
\mathbf{x}

does not belong to the domain of the environment
E
.
The domain of an environment
E
is written
\mathtt{dom}(E)
and
is defined recursively by
\mathtt{dom}(\epsilon):=\varnothing

and
\mathtt{dom}([x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E^{\prime}):=\{x\}\cup\mathtt{dom}(E^{\prime})
.

The main technical lemma is:

Lemma 6.1 (GLAMoUr simulation).

Let
s
be a state reachable from an initial state
whose focus is
t_{0}
and let
\mathcal{S}_{0}:=\mathsf{fv}(t_{0})
. Then:

1.

If
s\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{m}}}s^{\prime}
,
then
\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\mathsf{db}}\equiv\{\!\!\{s^{\prime}\}\!\!\}
.

2.

If
s\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{e}}}s^{\prime}
,
then
\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}\equiv\{\!\!\{s^{\prime}\}\!\!\}
.

3.

If
s\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{i}}s^{\prime}
,
then
\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}=\{\!\!\{s^{\prime}\}\!\!\}
, for all
i\in\{1..5\}
.

4.

If
s
is
\leadsto
-irreducible,
then
\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}
is
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}
-irreducible (progress property).

As a consequence, any
sequence
of GLAMoUr transitions
corresponds to a
sequence
of (stable)
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction steps
interleaved with equivalences.
To
postpone
all the intermediate structural equivalence steps
to obtain

\{\!\!\{s_{1}\}\!\!\}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}\equiv\{\!\!\{s_{n}\}\!\!\}
,
we need the following lemma, stating that
\equiv
is a

strong bisimulation
with respect to
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}
, thus
obtaining in particular a postponement property:

Lemma 6.2 (Strong bisimulation).

Let
t_{0},u_{0}
be stable terms such that

u_{0}\equiv t_{0}
.
If
t_{0}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t_{1}
, then there
exists
u_{1}
such that

u_{0}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}\equiv t_{1}
.

This result relies crucially on the stability notion introduced
earlier in this section.
For instance, suppose
s
is not rigid.
Then, the equivalence
t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]\equiv t[y\backslash s][x\backslash u]
cannot be postponed after the
(non-stable) step
t[y\backslash s][x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}t[y\backslash s][x\backslash u^{\prime}]
because the rules

esLA
∙
and
esLS
∙
cannot be applied to derive a step

t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}t[x\backslash u^{\prime}][y\backslash s]
, given that
s
is
not rigid.

High and Low-Level Implementation

We can now show the main results of this section.
For that, we first say that a term is
pure
if it contains no ESs.

Relying on the previous results, it can now be shown that reduction
in the fireball calculus (
\to_{\beta_{f}}
) can be implemented
through
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
(
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}
) with quadratic overhead in time.
More precisely, if we write
t^{\Downarrow}
for the
full
unfolding
of a term
t
, performing
all
the pending ESs in
t
:

Theorem 6.3 (High-Level Implementation).

Let
t
be a pure term and
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{fv}(t)
.
If
t\to_{\beta_{f}}^{n}t^{\prime}
, then there exists
u
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}^{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{{\footnotesize{$k$}}}}u
where
u^{\Downarrow}=t^{\prime}
and

k\in O(|t|\cdot(n^{2}+1))
.

Now, we show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
(
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}
) can be implemented in the GLAMoUr (
\leadsto
) with bilinear overhead in time, as a function of
the size of the starting term and the length of the reduction sequence
to normal form:

Theorem 6.4 (Low-Level Implementation).

Let
t
be a pure term.
If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}^{\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{{\footnotesize{$n$}}}}t^{\prime}
, with
t^{\prime}
in normal form
and
s
is an initial state such that
\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}=t
,
then
s\leadsto^{k}s^{\prime}
, where
\{\!\!\{s^{\prime}\}\!\!\}
is
structurally equivalent to
t^{\prime}
and
k\in O(|t|\cdot(n+1))
.

This result entails that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is time-invariant,
i.e.
, that it can
be simulated with at most polynomial overhead by a time-invariant
cost model (such as Turing machines or RAMs):

Corollary 6.5.

The
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
strategy is an invariant implementation of open CBV.

7.
A Quantitative Interpretation

In this section, we present a non-idempotent intersection type system
called
\mathcal{U}
(for
\mathcal{U}
seful).
We show that it is sound (
Theorem

7.4
) and complete
(
Theorem

7.7
) with respect to reduction in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
Therefore, we yield in
Corollary

7.8

that system
\mathcal{U}
characterises termination in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
:
a term
t
is
typable
in system
\mathcal{U}
(or

\mathcal{U}
-typable
) if and only if
t
terminates in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
In this sense, we say that system
\mathcal{U}
is a
quantitative
interpretation
of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.

7.1.
Defining System
\mathcal{U}

In quantitative type systems, one key feature is that a single
occurrence of an expression in the (static) source code of the program
may be (dynamically) used many times during runtime, each time playing
a different role, expressed by a different type.
For example, in some programs an occurrence
of the identity function
{\tt I}
may be applied twice, while in other
programs it may be applied only once. In each case, the type of the
subexpression
{\tt I}
should change to reflect this quantitative
difference. Hence
terms do not have a unique type
. In fact,
as usual in intersection type systems, there is no notion of

principal type
in
\mathcal{U}
.

To be able to capture quantitative information about evaluation,
the type of each
\lambda
-abstraction
is a
multiset
(rather than a
set
) whose cardinality
corresponds exactly to the number of times that the abstraction is
applied to some argument during the whole
evaluation process. In general, the type of
an abstraction is a multiset of the form
\mathcal{T}=[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}]
, where each of the
\alpha_{i}
is
an
arrow type
. For example, the underlined identity function
in the expression

(\lambda f.\,x\,(f\,y)\,(f\,z))\,\underline{{\tt I}}
takes
part in
two
function applications. Hence, one possible type
assignment for that subexpression is
\vdash{\tt I}:[(\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}),(\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S})]
, meaning that the identity
function is applied
twice
during evaluation,
once to an argument of type
\mathcal{T}
and once to an argument
of type
\mathcal{S}
. However, some abstractions may never be
applied. For example, the identity function in
the program
x[x\backslash{\tt I}]
does not take part in any function
application. These abstractions are typed with the
empty
multiset

[]
.

As studied in
Section

4
,
all (terminating) terms evaluate to either a
variable
,
an
abstraction
, or a
structure
.
Abstractions, as well as variables bound to abstractions,
are assigned finite multisets of arrow types, as we just said, called
arrow multi-types
.
Structures, and variables bound to structures, on the other hand,
are always given a distinguished type, just written
\,\mathbb{s}
.

Formally, the types of system
\mathcal{U}
are given by the
following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rlcl@{\sep\sep}rlcll}\text{{\bf(Arrow Types)}}&\alpha,\beta&\mathrel{::=}&\mathcal{T}^{?}\to\mathcal{T}\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\\
\text{{\bf(Arrow Multi-Types)}}&\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}&\mathrel{::=}&[\alpha_{i}]_{i\in I}\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt&\text{where $I$ is a finite set}\\
\text{{\bf(Types)}}&\mathcal{T},\mathcal{S}&\mathrel{::=}&\,\mathbb{s}\mid\mathcal{M}\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\\
\text{{\bf(Optional Types)}}&\mathcal{T}^{?},\mathcal{S}^{?}&\mathrel{::=}&\bot\ \mid\mathcal{T}\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\end{array}

We distinguish a set of
tight constants
given by

\mathbb{t}\mathrel{::=}\,\mathbb{s}\mid[]
, where
\,\mathbb{s}
is assigned to
terms evaluating to structures, while
[]
is assigned to terms
evaluating to abstractions that are not going to be applied.
Unlike in previous non-idempotent intersection type systems for CBV,
we distinguish the
optional
type
\bot
from
[]
, the
former meaning that
no typing information
is available, and
the latter meaning that it is typing an abstraction in a non-applied
position.

Typing environments

\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
are functions
mapping variables to optional types, assigning
\bot
to all but
finitely many variables.
The
domain
of an environment
\Gamma
is defined as

\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma):=\{x\mid\Gamma(x)\neq\bot\}
, and

\varnothing
denotes the empty typing environment, mapping every
variable to
\bot
.

Type assumptions
are denoted
x:\mathcal{T}^{?}
, meaning that the
environment assigns
\mathcal{T}^{?}
to
x
, and
\bot
to any other variable.

The
union of arrow multi-types
, written

\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathrel{\uplus}\mathcal{M}_{2}
, is a multiset of types defined as
expected, where
[]
is the neutral element, and
\mathrel{\uplus}
denotes
the union of multisets.
The
union of types
, written
