\mathcal{T}_{1}+\mathcal{T}_{2}
, is the
(associative)
partial
operation on types given by

\,\mathbb{s}+\,\mathbb{s}:=\,\mathbb{s}
and

\mathcal{M}_{1}+\mathcal{M}_{2}:=\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathrel{\uplus}\mathcal{M}_{2}
, with all
other cases undefined.
The
union of optional types
is given by

\bot+\bot:=\bot
,
\bot+\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}
, and

\mathcal{T}+\bot:=\mathcal{T}
, so that
\bot
is the neutral element.
For typing environments
(\Gamma_{i})_{i\in I}
, we write
+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}
for
the environment mapping each variable
x
to

+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}(x)
.
Additionally,
\Gamma+\Delta
and
\Gamma+_{j\in J}\Delta_{j}
are
particular instances of the general notation.
When
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)=\varnothing
we write

\Gamma;\Delta
instead of
\Gamma+\Delta
to emphasise that
the domains of
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are disjoint.
As a consequence,
\Gamma;x:\bot
is identical to
\Gamma
.

The
counting
function
{\tt ta}(\_)
returns the number of

t
op-level
a
rrows in a (optional) type, and it is defined
by
{\tt ta}(\,\mathbb{s}):=0
,
{\tt ta}(\bot):=0
, and

{\tt ta}([\alpha_{i}]_{i\in I}):=|I|
.
Note that
{\tt ta}(\mathcal{T}_{1}+\mathcal{T}_{2})={\tt ta}(\mathcal{T}_{1})+{\tt ta}(\mathcal{T}_{2})
.

We define a
controlled weakening
relation
\lhd
between
optional types and types by declaring that
\bot\lhd\mathbb{t}
and

\mathcal{T}\lhd\mathcal{T}
hold.

Typing judgements
in system
\mathcal{U}
are of the form

\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
, where the usual sequent notation of typing
judgements is now decorated with natural numbers
m
and
e

called
counters
.
Under appropriate conditions, these counters correspond
exactly

to the number of
\mathsf{db}
-steps (
m
) and
\mathsf{lsv}
-steps (
e
)
in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
that are required to reduce terms to normal form.

Typing rules
of system
\mathcal{U}
are:

\frac{n={\tt ta}(\mathcal{T})}{x:\mathcal{T}\vdash^{(0,n)}x:\mathcal{T}}\textup{{var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\left(\Gamma_{i};x:\mathcal{T}^{?}_{i}\vdash^{(m_{i},e_{i})}t:\mathcal{S}_{i}\right)_{i\in I}}{+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}\vdash^{(+_{i\in I}m_{i},+_{i\in I}e_{i})}\lambda x.\,t:[\mathcal{T}^{?}_{i}\to\mathcal{S}_{i}]_{i\in I}}\textup{{abs}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\,\mathbb{s}\hskip 8.5359pt\Delta\vdash^{(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})}u:\mathbb{t}}{\Gamma+\Delta\vdash^{(m+m^{\prime},e+e^{\prime})}t\,u:\,\mathbb{s}}\textup{{appP}}

\frac{\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:[\mathcal{T}^{?}\to\mathcal{S}]\hskip 8.5359pt\mathcal{T}^{?}\lhd\mathcal{T}\hskip 8.5359pt\Delta\vdash^{(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})}u:\mathcal{T}}{\Gamma+\Delta\vdash^{(1+m+m^{\prime},e+e^{\prime})}t\,u:\mathcal{S}}\textup{{appC}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\Gamma;x:\mathcal{T}^{?}\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{S}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{T}^{?}\lhd\mathcal{T}\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta\vdash^{(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})}u:\mathcal{T}}{\Gamma+\Delta\vdash^{(m+m^{\prime},e+e^{\prime})}t[x\backslash u]:\mathcal{S}}\textup{{es}}

A
(type) derivation
is a tree built by applying the previous
(inductive) typing rules.
We write
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
to denote a
type derivation
\Phi
ending in the typing judgement

\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
.
Sometimes we may write
\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
,
omitting the name
\Phi
.

Like most non-idempotent intersection type systems, system

\mathcal{U}
is inspired by Linear Logic
(
Girard, 1988
)
, and
specifically by the relational semantics for CBV in
(
Ehrhard, 2012
)
.
Indeed, a multiset
[\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}]
can be understood as
a multiplicative conjunction
\alpha_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{n}
.
Note also that there are no general
weakening
and
contraction

rules, though
\bot\lhd\mathbb{t}
and
\mathbb{t}+\mathbb{t}=\mathbb{t}
can
be understood respectively as controlled forms of weakening and contraction.

Rule
abs
is a standard rule in non-idempotent intersection
type systems for CBV, and reflects the
weak
nature of reduction
in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
since reduction does not proceed within abstractions.
For example, the derivation
\rhd\varnothing\vdash\lambda x.\,\Omega:[]

is valid even if
\Omega
is non-terminating.

Rule
es
can be easily derived from rules
abs
and
appC
.
Rules for applications follow the
consuming/persistent paradigm

(
Kesner and Vial, 2020
;
Kesner and Viso, 2022
)

and thus deserve some discussion.
Indeed, system
\mathcal{U}
tracks different natures of the
application constructors involved in the evaluation process of a program:
a term constructor is
consuming
if it is
destroyed
during
evaluation, whereas a
persistent
constructor
remains
as
a syntactical subterm of the normal form.
For example, given the reduction to normal form

x((\lambda y.\,y)z)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}x(y[y\backslash z])
,
the leftmost application constructor is persistent, while the rightmost is consuming.
Hence, rule
appP
(resp.
appC
) is used to type persistent
(resp. consuming) applications.
More specifically,
appP
requires the left-hand side of the
application to have type
\,\mathbb{s}
, ensuring that no redex will be created,
so the (typed) application constructor is persistent.
Rule
appC
requires the left-hand side of the application to type
with an arrow multi-type, ensuring a
\mathsf{db}
-redex is created at some
point during evaluation, and thus the first counter is incremented.

As just explained, system
\mathcal{U}
uses the consuming/persistent
paradigm exclusively to type applications.
This approach contrasts with more radical uses for CBV, such as
(
Kesner and Viso, 2022
)
,
which apply the same paradigm to type
all
constructors, thereby
increasing the number of typing rules and making the typing system more
difficult to use.

We now discuss the use of the controlled weakening relation in the
typing rules
appC
and
es
.
In CBV, the arguments of applications and closures are always
evaluated. Since system
\mathcal{U}
intends to characterise
termination in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, all arguments must necessarily be typed.
For example, in
(\lambda x.\,{\tt I})\,(z\,z)
the
subexpression
z\,z
is a structure of type
\,\mathbb{s}
,
so one would like to type the body of the abstraction
as
x:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash{\tt I}:\mathcal{T}
.
But it is impossible to derive this judgement because
x
does not
occur free in
{\tt I}
.
This is why we introduce the controlled weakening relation as a premise
in rules
appC
and
es
.
In our example, one can give a “stronger” type to
\lambda x.\,{\tt I}
,
namely
[[]\to\mathcal{T}]
, using the fact that
\bot\lhd\,\mathbb{s}
.
A similar use of controlled weakening for the rule
es
is shown
below, where it should be recalled that the empty typing environment

\varnothing
is identical to
\varnothing;x:\bot
.

Assuming that
\bot\lhd\,\mathbb{s}
,
one typing derivation that illustrates the use of subsumption is the following:

\frac{\frac{}{\varnothing;x:\bot\vdash^{(0,0)}{\tt I}:[]}\quad\textsc{Right = {{abs}} }\hskip 14.22636pt\bot\lhd\,\mathbb{s}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\frac{}{z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,0)}z:\,\mathbb{s}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right = {{var}} }\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,0)}z:\,\mathbb{s}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right = {{var}} }}{z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,0)}z\,z:\,\mathbb{s}}\quad\textsc{Right = {{appP}} }}{z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,0)}{\tt I}[x\backslash z\,z]:\,\mathbb{s}}\textup{{es}}

We end this section by mentioning some basic properties and notions
of the type system
\mathcal{U}
.

Relevance.

The fact that axioms in system
\mathcal{U}
are not weakened and that
rules are
multiplicative
yields that system
\mathcal{U}
is

relevant
. Formally:

Lemma 7.1 (Relevance).

If
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
,
then
\mathsf{rv}(t)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)\subseteq\mathsf{fv}(t)
.

Note that
\mathsf{fv}(t)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
does not always hold.
For instance,
\rhd\varnothing\vdash\lambda x.\,y:[]
but

\{y\}\nsubseteq\varnothing
.

Appropriateness.

To reason inductively about typing derivations, it is sometimes
necessary to ensure invariants for the types of the free variables in
a term.
For example, for a term like
t[x\backslash{\tt I}]
, we may need to track
that
x
is bound to an abstraction when applying the inductive
hypothesis for the subterm
t
.
Specifically, we say that a typing environment
\Gamma
is

appropriate
with respect to an abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
,
written
\mathtt{appropriate}_{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma)
, if for each
x\in\mathcal{A}
,
we have
\Gamma(x)\neq\,\mathbb{s}
.
In other words,
\Gamma(x)
must be
\bot
or an arrow multi-type
\mathcal{M}
.
For instance,
\mathtt{appropriate}_{\{x\}}(x:[],y:\,\mathbb{s})

holds, while
\mathtt{appropriate}_{\{x\}}(x:\,\mathbb{s},y:\,\mathbb{s})

does not.

Tightness.

Typing in system
\mathcal{U}
not only characterises termination in

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, but it is also capable of providing
exact measures
for
the length of reduction sequences to normal form.
To state this formally, we define a subset of typing derivations
called
tight derivations
, as follows.
A type is
tight
if it is a tight constant
\mathbb{t}
.
An optional type
\mathcal{T}^{?}
is
tight
if it is either
\bot
or
a tight type.
A typing environment
\Gamma
is
tight
if
\Gamma(x)
is
tight for every variable
x
.
A typing judgement
\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
is
tight
if
both
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{T}
are tight.
A derivation
\Phi
of a typing judgement is
tight
if the
judgement
\Phi
derives is tight.
For example, the derivable judgement
x:[]\vdash^{(0,0)}x:[]

is tight: the counters
(0,0)
indicate that the evaluating
x

requires no function application or substitution steps, meaning that

x
is already in normal form.
In contrast, the derivable judgement

x:[\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}]\vdash^{(0,1)}x:[\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}]

is
not
tight, and the counters
(0,1)
provide an upper bound
on the number of steps required for evaluating
x
.

Example 7.2.

Let

t=(x\,z)[x\backslash y[y\backslash{\tt I}]]
.
Taking
\mathcal{T}:=[\,\mathbb{s}\to\,\mathbb{s}]
,
the following typing derivation
\Phi
turns out to be tight:

\begin{array}[b]{c@{}r}\hskip-5.69046pt\frac{\frac{}{x:\mathcal{T}\vdash^{(0,1)}x:\mathcal{T}}\textup{{var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,0)}z:\,\mathbb{s}}\textup{{var}}}{z:\,\mathbb{s},x:\mathcal{T}\vdash^{(1,1)}x\,z:\,\mathbb{s}}\textup{{appC}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\frac{}{y:\mathcal{T}\vdash^{(0,1)}y:\mathcal{T}}\textup{{var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\frac{}{w:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,0)}w:\,\mathbb{s}}\textup{{var}}}{\varnothing\vdash^{(0,0)}{\tt I}:\mathcal{T}}\textup{{abs}}}{\varnothing\vdash^{(0,1)}y[y\backslash{\tt I}]:\mathcal{T}}\textup{{es}}\\
&\,\textup{{es}}\\
\cline{1-1}\cr\\
z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(1,2)}(x\,z)[x\backslash y[y\backslash{\tt I}]]:\,\mathbb{s}\end{array}

where
\,\mathbb{s}\lhd\,\mathbb{s}
and

\mathcal{T}=[\,\mathbb{s}\to\,\mathbb{s}]\lhd[\,\mathbb{s}\to\,\mathbb{s}]=\mathcal{T}

hold.
Note that all the other subderivations of
\Phi
are not tight,
except for the derivation corresponding to the judgement

z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(0,1)}z:\,\mathbb{s}
.

Tightness is essential for proving that the system
\mathcal{U}
is
sound and complete with respect to
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
(
Theorems

7.4
and
7.7
).

7.2.
Soundness and Completeness of System
\mathcal{U}

We first address the
soundness
of system
\mathcal{U}
with
respect to
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
,
i.e.
, typability in system
\mathcal{U}
implies

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
-termination.
Additionally, our soundness result provides exact quantitative
information about the reduction steps to normal form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
More precisely, we show that when a term
t
is tightly typable in
system
\mathcal{U}
with counters
(m,e)
, then
t
evaluates
in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
to a normal form
u
in exactly
m

\mathsf{db}
-steps
and exactly
e

\mathsf{lsv}
-steps.

The proof of soundness follows well-understood techniques
(
Bucciarelli et al., 2017
)
:
it requires a subject reduction property, based in turn on a
substitution lemma (omitted here; see
Appendix

E
).
Due to the
contextual
nature of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, these lemmas are not
trivial, as they require formulating complex invariants on the
contextual parameters
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mu
that define

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.

We begin by showing that any
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
step preserves typing and
correctly decrements the counters.

Proposition 7.3 (Subject Reduction).

Let
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
, where

\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}

and
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
, where

\mathtt{appropriate}_{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma)
.
Suppose moreover that if
\mu=@
, then either

\mathcal{T}=\,\mathbb{s}
or
\mathcal{T}
is a singleton, i.e., of the form
[\alpha]
.
Then, there exists a typing derivation
\Phi^{\prime}
such that

\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})}t^{\prime}:\mathcal{T}
,
where if
\rho=\mathsf{db}
, then
m>0
and
(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})=(m-1,e)
,
and if
\rho=\mathsf{lsv}
, then
e>0
and
(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})=(m,e-1)
.

We can now state the result of quantitative soundness: a tight
derivation of a term
t
with counters
(m,e)
guarantees
weakly terminating in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, with exactly
m

\mathsf{db}
-steps
and
e

\mathsf{lsv}
-steps.

Theorem 7.4 (Quantitative Soundness of System
\mathcal{U}
).

Let
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{fv}(t)
, and
let
\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
be a tight
derivation in system
\mathcal{U}
.
Then, there exists a
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}
-irreducible term
u
such
that
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}^{m+e}u
, where
m
and
e

are respectively the number of
\mathsf{db}
-steps and
\mathsf{lsv}
-steps
in the reduction sequence.

To illustrate this property, consider the tight derivation
\Phi

for
t=(x\,z)[x\backslash y[y\backslash{\tt I}]]
in

Example

7.2
, where the counter in the conclusion
of
\Phi
is
(1,2)
. A reduction sequence from
t
terminates in

x_{1}[x_{1}\backslash z][x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\{z\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}

(see
Example

4.3
).
The first counter, 1, matches the number of
\mathsf{db}
-steps in the
reduction sequence, while the second counter, 2, corresponds to the
number of
\mathsf{lsv}
-steps.

Note that non-tight derivations may not provide exact information
about the length of reduction sequences to normal form.
For example, the subderivation

\rhd z:\,\mathbb{s};x:\mathcal{T}\vdash^{(1,1)}x\,z:\,\mathbb{s}

in
Example

7.2
is not tight, as
\mathcal{T}
is not
tight, and the counters
(1,1)
do not correspond to the number of
steps needed to reach the normal form of
x\,z
which is
already in normal form.

We now address the
completeness
of system
\mathcal{U}

with respect to
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
: termination in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
implies

\mathcal{U}
-typability.
Additionally, our result provides exact quantitative information
about the reduction steps to normal form in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.

Completeness of quantitative type systems can also be proved by
following standard techniques, requiring a subject expansion property
based in turn on an anti-substitution lemma (omitted here; see
Appendix

E
).
These statements are quite technical because the properties must be
generalised appropriately to reason inductively.

First, we guarantee that normal forms are tightly typable in system

\mathcal{U}
.
For that, we construct tight environments for each normal form

t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, by typing the
reachable

variables in
\mathcal{A}
with
[]
and those in
\mathcal{S}
with
\,\mathbb{s}
.
More precisely, given
t
,
\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{S}
such that

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
, the
tight environment
for
t
under

(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
is written
\mathsf{TEnv}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)
and defined as the
environment
\Gamma
such that
\Gamma(x)=[]
when

x\in\mathcal{A}\cap\mathsf{rv}(t)
,
\Gamma(x)=\,\mathbb{s}
when

x\in\mathcal{S}\cap\mathsf{rv}(t)
, and
\Gamma(x)=\bot
otherwise.

Tight environments allow us to type normal forms
tightly
:

Proposition 7.5 (Normal Forms are Tight Typable).

Let
t
be a term in
\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
.
Then, there exists a tight type
\mathbb{t}
such that

\Phi\rhd\mathsf{TEnv}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)\vdash^{(0,0)}t:\mathbb{t}
.
Moreover, if
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
then
\mathbb{t}=[]
, and if

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then
\mathbb{t}=\,\mathbb{s}
.

Subject Expansion is analogous to
Proposition 7.3 (Subject Reduction).
,
but applies in the
reverse direction
: given a typable term

t^{\prime}
and a reduction step
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
the term
t
is typable as well, with the same type and typing environment.

Proposition 7.6 (Subject Expansion).

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
hold, and suppose

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}

where
\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}
and

\Phi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m^{\prime},e^{\prime})}t^{\prime}:\mathcal{T}
, with
\mathtt{appropriate}_{\mathcal{A}}(\Gamma)
.
Furthermore, assume that if
\mu=@
, then either

\mathcal{T}=\,\mathbb{s}
or
\mathcal{T}
is a singleton, i.e., of the form
[\alpha]
.
Then, there exists a derivation
\Phi
such that

\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathcal{T}
, where
if
\rho=\mathsf{db}
, then
(m,e)=(m^{\prime}+1,e^{\prime})
, and
if
\rho=\mathsf{lsv}
, then
(m,e)=(m^{\prime},e^{\prime}+1)
.

We now state a quantitative version of completeness of system
\mathcal{U}
.
That is, given a weakly terminating
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
reduction sequence from

t
containing exactly
m

\mathsf{db}
-steps and exactly
e

\mathsf{lsv}
-steps, there exists a tight derivation of
t
with
counters
(m,e)
.

Theorem 7.7 (Quantitative Completeness of System
\mathcal{U}
).

Let
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{fv}(t)
and consider a reduction sequence

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}^{n}u
, where
u
is
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathtt{top},\mathcal{S}}
-irreducible.
Let
n=m+e
, where
m
and
e
are respectively the number
of
\mathsf{db}
and
\mathsf{lsv}
steps in the sequence.
Then, there exists a tight derivation
\Phi
, a tight environment

\Gamma
, and a tight type
\mathbb{t}
such that

\Phi\rhd\Gamma\vdash^{(m,e)}t:\mathbb{t}
.

To illustrate this result, take the reduction sequence starting from

t=(x\,z)[x\backslash y[y\backslash{\tt I}]]
and
ending in
x_{1}[x_{1}\backslash z][x\backslash y][y\backslash{\tt I}]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\{z\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
(see
Example

4.3
).
The length of this sequence is
3=m+e
, with
m=1

\mathsf{db}
-step and
e=2

\mathsf{lsv}
-steps.

Example

7.2
shows a tight derivation
\Phi
,
a tight environment
z:\,\mathbb{s}
, and a tight type
\,\mathbb{s}

such that
\Phi\rhd z:\,\mathbb{s}\vdash^{(1,2)}t:\,\mathbb{s}
.

Combining
Theorems

7.4
and
7.7
we get
that a term
t
is typable in system
\mathcal{U}
if and only if
it is
weakly
terminating in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
The characterisation of termination through typability follows from
these theorems and
Corollary

4.6
.
Moreover, this characterisation is quantitative:

Corollary 7.8 (Quantitative Characterisation of Termination).

A term
t
is tight typable with counters
(m,e)
in system

\mathcal{U}
if and only if
t
terminates in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
after
m

\mathsf{db}
-steps and
e

\mathsf{lsv}
-steps.

8.
Conclusions

This work contributes to the study of useful call-by-value in two
different ways.
At a
syntactic
level, we propose an inductive specification of
usefulness for open CBV evaluation called
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, which contrasts
with previous notions of usefulness (both for CBN and CBV) that are
defined by inductive means.
Our specification is inspired by the specification of strong
call-by-need evaluation in
(
Balabonski et al., 2017
)
.
Although these strategies are very different, one point in common is
that they crucially depend on the information of the surrounding
evaluation context, reflected in the form of parameters.
We think that this technique can scale to other evaluation strategies,
and in particular that it could be applied to provide an inductive
formulation of useful call-by-name evaluation, originally formulated
non-inductively in
(
Accattoli and Lago, 2014
)
.
Moreover, we also show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
and
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
are formally related
through a suitable unfolding operation.
Our last contribution at a syntactic level is establishing a relation
between
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
and an existing definition of usefulness in the
literature, the GLAMoUr abstract machine from
(
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
)
.

At a
semantic
level, we propose the first model of useful
evaluation in the literature, based on a non-idempotent intersection
type system we dub system
\mathcal{U}
.
Even though the specification of the operational semantics of useful
CBV evaluation is highly complex, system
\mathcal{U}
is notably
simple.
This highlights the ability of semantic approaches based on
non-idempotent intersection types to capture intricate operational
details with streamlined intuitive methods.
System
\mathcal{U}
characterises termination of the
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
, and
provides exact quantitative information for the length of reduction
sequences to normal form. This is why system
\mathcal{U}
is a

quantitative
interpretation of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
Our contribution is novel, as previous definitions of useful
evaluation (including useful CBV) in the literature lack semantic
models, and current quantitative interpretations of CBV do not
consider usefulness.
Moreover, with the scarce exception of
(
Kesner and Viso, 2022
)
, entirely
based on the (verbose) persistent/consuming paradigm, this is the
only “simple” and “concise” quantitative type system for CBV that is able to count substitution steps exactly.

Several complementary properties are worth studying.
A challenging question is how to extend our inductive
specification of usefulness to
strong
CBV so that evaluation
is also allowed inside abstractions.
This is relevant for the implementation of proof assistants based on
dependent type theory, in which type checking requires deciding the
definitional equality of type expressions up to full
\beta
-conversion,
thus requiring strong evaluation.
Moreover, we would apply the semantic techniques used for open CBV and yield a quantitative type system for useful strong CBV.
Even more challenging would be to adapt all this technology to
call-by-need, and to call-by-push-value.
Moreover, we would like to understand the flexibility of system

\mathcal{U}
, particularly its potential to capture

other optimisations
of CBV, such as those in
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri, 2019
)
.

Supplementary Material

In this appendix we use the following complementary notations:

If
X,Y
are sets, we write
X\mathrel{\#}Y
to mean that
X
and
Y
are

disjoint
,
i.e.
,
X\cap Y=\varnothing
.

We write
\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{L})
for the
domain
of
\mathtt{L}
,
i.e.
,

\mathtt{dom}([x_{1}\backslash t_{1}]\ldots[x_{n}\backslash t_{n}]):=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
.

Appendix A
Proofs of Section
3
“Linear Call-by-Value”

In this section, we present the proofs for the lemmas characterising
normal forms in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.

Drawing inspiration from Balabonski, Lanco, and Melquiond’s work
in
[
Balabonski et al., 2023
]
, we obtain an
inductive
syntactic
characterisation of normal forms in
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
This inductive definition uses two parameters: a
value frame

\mathcal{V}
, which is a set of variables, and a
positional flag

\mu\in\{@,{\text{$\not$\!@}}\}
.
Both parameters encode information about the evaluation context in
which a term is considered a normal form.
Specifically, the set
\mathcal{V}
tracks variables bound to a value in
the context, while
\mu
identifies whether subterms appear in
applied or non-applied positions within the evaluation context.
For example,
x
appears in an applied position in the term

x\,y
, while
y
appears in a non-applied position.
For top-level terms, the positional flag is always
\not
​@
.
The set of
normal forms
under
\mathcal{V}
and
\mu
, written

\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
, is inductively defined as follows:

\frac{x\notin\mathcal{V}}{x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{\lambda x.\,t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-lam${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},@}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}{t\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}}\textup{{NF-app${}^{\circ}$}}

\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\},\mu}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{Val}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esVal${}^{\circ}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pt\neg(u\in\mathsf{Val})}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esNonVal${}^{\circ}$}}

where the predicate
t\in\mathsf{Val}
states whether
t
is of the form

v\mathtt{L}
or not.

Value frames are sets of variables that are bound to values, so in
rule
NF-esVal
∘
, we extend the value frame of the left premise with
the variable bound by the ES.
Accordingly, in rule
NF-var
∘
, the variable
x
must not be in
the value frame; otherwise it would mean that it must be substituted
by some value during computation.
As an example,

x[x\backslash y]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}y[x\backslash y]

can be derived using the inductive rule
lsv
∘
because

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,y)}}y
, so intuitively

x[x\backslash y]\notin\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
However,
y[x\backslash y]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
as we
state below:

\frac{\frac{}{y\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\circ}$}}\frac{}{y\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\circ}$}}}{y[x\backslash y]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-esVal${}^{\circ}$}}

If we wanted to derive a judgement

x[x\backslash y]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
, we would end
up with a premise stating
x\notin\{x\}
, which is false.
In the preceeding derivation tree, the predicate

y\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
indicates that
y
is in
normal form with respect to a value frame containing
x
.
This dependency comes from the reduction step

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,y)}}y
.

Just as normal forms are parameterised by a value frame
\mathcal{V}
,
representing the set of variables that are bound to values, evaluation
must also be generalised by parameterising it with value frame, to
establish a precise relation between reduction and normal forms.
Consequently, we define the set of

reduction rules related to a value frame
\mathcal{V}
as follows:

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{V}}:=\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}\cup\{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}\ |\ x\in\mathcal{V}\}

A term
t
belongs to the set
\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
of

reducible terms under a value frame
\mathcal{V}
if there is a
step kind
\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{V}}
and a term
t^{\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}
;
t
belongs to the set
\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
of

irreducible terms under
\mathcal{V}
if
t\notin\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Lemma A.1 (Weakening lemma).

Let
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
and let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
be such that

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\mathrel{\#}\mathsf{rv}(t)
.
Then,
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mu}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by induction on the derivation of
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
.
∎

We can now show the two main results.
First, we show soundness of the syntactic characterisation of normal
forms with respect to the parametrised reduction rules.
More specifically, we show that given any value frame
\mathcal{V}
and any
positional flag
\mu
, a term in normal form under
\mathcal{V}
and

\mu
is in the set
\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Lemma A.2.

For any
\mathcal{V}
,
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\notin\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},@}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.
∎

Proposition A.3 (Soundness of linear normal forms).

If
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
, then
t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
.

1.

NF-var
∘
.
Then,
t=x
and

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 8.98679pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty x\notin\mathcal{V}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=41.46657pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{NF-var${}^{\circ}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}$}}}}\end{array}

The only rule that would allow us to reduce
x
is
sub
∘
, as
follows:
x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}v
with
x\in\mathcal{V}
.
But it is impossible since the premise of rule
NF-var
∘
states
that
x\notin\mathcal{V}
.
We then conclude with
x\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

2.

NF-lam
∘
.
Then,
t=\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
,
with
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
.
There are no rules to reduce abstractions, so it is immediate to
conclude with
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

3.

NF-app
∘
.
Then,
t=u\,s
and

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},@}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=97.08708pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{NF-app${}^{\circ}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 24.62854pt\hbox{$\displaystyle u\,s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}$}}}}\end{array}

Three rules would allow us to reduce
u\,s
:

db
∘
,
appL
∘
, and
appR
∘
.
We show that none of these apply:

•

We cannot apply rule
db
∘
to conclude

u\,s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
, because it would imply

u\in\mathsf{Abs}
at the same time that

u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},@}
, which is impossible by

Lemma

A.2
.

•

By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield
u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
appL
∘
to conclude with

u\,s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

•

By the
i.h.
on
s
, we yield
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
appR
∘
to conclude with

u\,s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

And we are done since
u\,s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
by
definition.

4.

NF-esVal
∘
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]
and

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{Val}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=151.9853pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{NF-esVal${}^{\circ}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 42.55338pt\hbox{$\displaystyle u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}$}}}}\end{array}

Three rules would allow us to reduce
u[x\backslash s]
:

lsv
∘
,
esL
∘
, and
esR
∘
.
We show that none of these apply:

•

By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield

u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}}
.
Then, we cannot apply rules
lsv
∘
and
esL
∘
to conclude
with
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

•

By the
i.h.
on
s
, we yield

s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, we cannot conclude
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

And we are done since
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
by
definition.

5.

NF-esNonVal
∘
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]
and

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pt\neg(s\in\mathsf{Val})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=148.17276pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{NF-esNonVal${}^{\circ}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 40.64711pt\hbox{$\displaystyle u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}$}}}}\end{array}

Three rules would allow us to reduce
u[x\backslash s]
:

lsv
∘
,
esL
∘
, and
esR
∘
.
We show that case
lsv
∘
does not apply, as the remaining cases
are analogous to the previous item.

Since
\neg(s\in\mathsf{Val})
, then we cannot conclude

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
by rule
lsv
∘
.

∎

Completeness intuitively states that any term in
\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
is
in normal form under the same
\mathcal{V}
and any
positional flag
\mu
.
However, we need to consider the set
\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
with caution,
since given an irreducible abstraction in an applied position, the
normal form predicate would fail, as it means that the computation of
the whole term can continue with a
\mathsf{db}
-step. Formally,

Lemma A.4.

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t^{\prime}

then for every value
w
there exists
t^{\prime\prime}

such that
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)}}t^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma A.5.

Let
x\notin\mathcal{V}
.
If
t\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
, then
t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
,
where
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}
if
u\in\mathsf{Val}
, and

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}
otherwise.

Proof.

By definition, there exists a step kind

\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
and a term
t^{\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}t^{\prime}
. We have two cases, depending on whether

x\in\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
or not:

•

If
x\in\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
, then
\rho=\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)}
,
with
y\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
We have two subcases:

–

If
x=y
, then
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)}}t^{\prime}
,
and we have to analyze the form of
u
:

*

If
u\in\mathsf{Val}
, then we can apply rule
lsv
∘
and
conclude with
t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

*

If
\neg(u\in\mathsf{Val})
, then
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}
.
This case is impossible since
x\notin\mathcal{V}
by hypothesis
but at the same time
x=y\in\mathcal{V}
.

–

If
x\neq y
, then
x\in\mathsf{fv}(w)
.
Let
w^{\prime}
be a value such that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(w^{\prime})
.
We can then apply
Lemma

A.4
, yielding

t^{\prime\prime}
such that
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w^{\prime})}}t^{\prime\prime}
.
Applying rule
esL
∘
, we derive

t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w^{\prime})}}t^{\prime\prime}[x\backslash u]
,
with
y\in\mathcal{V}
,
so that
t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

•

If
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
, then we can apply rule
esL
∘
to
reduce
t[x\backslash u]
, so we conclude with

t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

∎

Proposition A.6 (Completeness of linear normal forms).

If
t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
and
(t\in\mathsf{Abs}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}})
,
then
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.

1.

t=x
.
The only rule that would reduce
x
is
sub
∘
.
By hypothesis,
x\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
so that
x\notin\mathcal{V}

by the definition of
\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, by rule
NF-var
∘
, we derive
x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
.

2.

t=\lambda x.\,u
.
Then
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
by the hypothesis, so applying rule

NF-lam
∘
we derive
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.

3.

t=u\,s
.
We necessarily have
u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
, since otherwise

u\,s
would by in
\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
by rule
appL
∘
.
Moreover,
\neg u\in\mathsf{Abs}
, because otherwise
u\,s

would reduce by the rule
db
∘
.
By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield (1)
u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},@}
.

Likewise, we necessarily have
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
, since
otherwise
u\,s
would by in
\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
by rule

appR
∘
.
Then, (2)
s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
by the
i.h.
on
s
.
Applying rule
NF-app
∘
with (1) and (2) as premises, we derive

u\,s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
.

4.

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
By
\alpha
-conversion, we assume
x\notin\mathcal{V}
.
We necessarily have
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
, since otherwise

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
by rule
esR
∘
.
Then, (1)
s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
by the
i.h.
on
s
.
We have to analyze two cases, depending on whether
u\in\mathsf{Val}

holds or not. Both cases are analogous, so we only focus on the case
where
u\in\mathsf{Val}
holds.
Since
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
, then

u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}}
by contraposition of

Lemma

A.5
.
Moreover,
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
, so the implication in the
hypothesis trivially holds.
Therefore, we can apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding
(2)
u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\},\mu}
.
Applying rule
NF-esVal
∘
to (1) and (2), we derive

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
.

∎

The following corollary combines the results of soundness and
completeness, so that the set of terms that are in normal form
according to the inductive predicate
\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}
is exactly
the set
\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Recall that the parameters
\mathcal{V}
and
\mu
are used in the
definition of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
to define evaluation
inductively
.
However, we are actually interested in the
top-level
case,
i.e.
,
in the evaluation of an
isolated
term.
In the case of top-level term, the value frame
\mathcal{V}
is empty,
because there is no surrounding context binding any variable, and the
positional flag
\mu
is taken to be non-applied (
\not
​@
),
because an isolated term is never applied.

Corollary A.7 (Characterisation of linear normal forms).

t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
iff
t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\varnothing}
.

An example of this characterisation is the term
y[x\backslash y]

we showed at the beginning of the section: it is in

\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
, as shown above, and it is in

\mathsf{Irred}^{\circ}_{\varnothing}
, since none of the rules in
\mathcal{R}_{\varnothing}
are
applicable to it.

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
4
“Useful Call-by-Value”

This section of the appendix is organised in two subsections:
in
Section

B.1
, we give an inductive
characterisation of normal forms.
In
Section

B.2
, we show that
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
enjoys the diamond property.

B.1.
Normal Forms for Useful Open Call-by-Value

This section provides a syntactic characterisation of
normal forms of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
,
together with their corresponding soundness and completeness results
(
Corollary

B.11
).

Characterising normal forms for useful evaluation is not simple
[
Accattoli and Leberle, 2022
]
.
Our inductive characterisation is similar to the one given for the

\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
calculus, in that it uses parameters.
Specifically, we use an abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
, a structure frame

\mathcal{S}
, and a positional flag
\mu\in\{@,{\text{$\not$\!@}}\}
.
These three parameters give information about the evaluation context
in which the (sub)term is considered to be a normal form.
As explained before in
Section

4
, the frames
\mathcal{A}
and

\mathcal{S}
track variables that are hereditary abstractions or
structures, respectively, whereas the positional flag tracks applied
positions of subterms with respect to the context.
For the top-level terms, the positional element is always in a
non-applied position (
i.e.
,
\not
​@
), as in the characterisation
of normal forms of
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
.
The set of
normal forms
of
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
under
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
,
and
\mu
, also called
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu)
-normal forms
,
is written
\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, and is defined inductively as:

\frac{x\in\mathcal{A}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}}{x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{}{\lambda x.\,t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-lam${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}{t\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-app${}^{\bullet}$}}

\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esA${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esS${}^{\bullet}$}}

If a variable is an hereditary abstraction (
x\in\mathcal{A}
) and it
is applied (
\mu=@
), substituting the variable contributes
to creating a
\mathsf{db}
-redex, so the variable is not in normal form.
Otherwise, it is in normal form, according to rule
NF-var
∙
.
For example, the term
x[x\backslash{\tt I}]
is in normal form if
non-applied (
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
), whereas it reduces to

{\tt I}[x\backslash{\tt I}]
if applied (
\mu=@
).
To derive
x[x\backslash{\tt I}]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\varnothing,@}
, we would
end up with a premise stating

x\in\{x\}\Rightarrow@={\text{$\not$\!@}}
, which does not hold.

Abstractions are in normal form if they are not applied.
In rule
NF-esA
∙
(resp.
NF-esS
∙
), the abstraction
(resp. structure) frame of the left premise is extended with the
bound variable in the ES, given that it is bound to an hereditary
abstraction (resp. structure), exactly as in the left premises of the
reduction rule
esLA
∙
(resp.
esLS
∙
).

We can now present the two main results of this section.
The first one is soundness of the syntactic characterisation of
normal forms with respect to the parametrized reduction rules.
More specifically, we state that given any abstraction and structure
frames
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{S}
and a positional flag
\mu
, a term
in normal form under these parameters belongs to the set

\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
of irreducible terms.

Lemma B.1.

For any
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{S}
,

(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\notin\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.
∎

Lemma B.2.

If
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
,
then either
t\notin\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
or
t\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.

•

t=x
.
Since
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
,

x
cannot be simultaneously in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{S}
,
so either
x\notin\mathcal{A}
and
x\notin\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
or
x\notin\mathcal{S}
and
x\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

t=\lambda x.\,u
.
Immediate, since
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
cannot be
derived by any rule, thus
\lambda x.\,u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

t=u\,s
.
Immediate, since
u\,s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
cannot be
derived by any rule, thus
u\,s\notin\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
To show that
t\notin\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
or
t\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
we assume
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and argue that
t\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We can assume
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
by
\alpha
-conversion.
We have the following subcases, depending on the rule used to
derive
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
:

–

h-sub
1
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
x\notin\mathcal{A}
.
By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield
u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Therefore,
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
cannot be
derived by rule
s-sub
1
.
Similarly, since
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
, then

u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}
by the
i.h.
on
u
.
Therefore,
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
cannot be
derived using rule
s-sub
2
.

–

h-sub
2
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
,
x\notin\mathcal{A}
,
and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
hold.
Since
(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
, then

u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by the
i.h.
on
u
.
Therefore,
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
cannot be
derived using rule
s-sub
1
.

On the other hand,
s\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by the
i.h.
on

s
, so
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
cannot
be derived using rule
s-sub
2
.

We thus conclude with
u[x\backslash s]\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

∎

Proposition B.3 (Soundness of Useful Normal Forms).

If
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
and
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
,
then
t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

•

NF-var
∙
.
Then
x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, with premise

x\in\mathcal{A}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
.
The only rule for reducing variables is
sub
∙
,
with the condition that
x\in\mathcal{A}
.
This implies by hypothesis that
\mu
must be
\not
​@
, contrary
to the request of rule
sub
∙
that
\mu=@
.
Hence, no reduction rule applies and we are done.

•

NF-lam
∙
.
Immediate, since
t
is an abstraction, and there are no rules
for reducing terms of this form.

•

NF-app
∙
.
Then,
t=u\,s
and

\frac{u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}{u\,s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-app${}^{\bullet}$}}

Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u\,s)}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
There are three rules for reducing applications:
db
∙
,
appL
∙
,
and
appR
∙
.
We show that none of these apply:

–

We cannot apply rule
db
∙
to conclude

u\,s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, because it would
imply that
u\in\mathsf{Abs}
at the same time that

u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
, which contradicts
Lemma

B.1
.

–

By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield
u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
appL
∙
to conclude with

u\,s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

–

By the
i.h.
on
s
, we yield
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
appR
∙
to conclude with

u\,s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

And we are done since
u\,s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

by definition.

•

NF-esA
∙
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]
and

\frac{u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esA${}^{\bullet}$}}

We assume
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
by
\alpha
-conversion.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
There are four rules for reducing closures:
lsv
∙
,
esR
∙
,

esLA
∙
, and
esLS
∙
.
We show that none of these apply:

–

By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield

u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.
Then, we cannot apply rules
lsv
∙
and
esLA
∙
to
conclude with
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

–

By the
i.h.
on
s
, we yield

s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
esR
∙
to conclude with

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

–

Since
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
holds by the invariant,
and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
holds by the hypothesis,
then
s\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by
Lemma

B.2
.
Hence, we cannot apply rule
esLS
∙
to conclude with

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

And we are done since
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

by definition.

•

NF-esS
∙
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]
and

\frac{u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esS${}^{\bullet}$}}

We assume
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
by
\alpha
-conversion.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
There are four rules for reducing closures:
lsv
∙
,
esR
∙
,

esLA
∙
, and
esLS
∙
.
We show that none of these apply:

–

We cannot apply rule
lsv
∙
to conclude

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, because
it would imply that
s=v\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{S}}
, while
at the same time
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by hypothesis,
which contradicts
Lemma

B.2
, as
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}

holds by the invariant.

–

By the
i.h.
on
s
, we yield

s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
esR
∙
to conclude with

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

–

By the
i.h.
on
u
, we yield

u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}
.
Then, we cannot apply rule
esLS
∙
to conclude with

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

–

Since
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
holds, and
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}

holds by the hypothesis, then
s\notin\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
by

Lemma

B.2
.
Hence, we cannot apply rule
esLA
∙
to conclude with

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

And we are done since
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

by definition.

∎

We now yield the lemmas necessary to achieve completeness of the
inductive definition of normal forms (
Proposition

B.10
).

Lemma B.4.

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
.
If
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, then

t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Furthermore, if
\mu=@
, then
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

1.

NF-var
∙
.
Then

\frac{x\in\mathcal{A}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}}{x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\bullet}$}}

Given
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},x)}
, we analyse two cases depending on
whether
x\in\mathcal{A}
or
x\in\mathcal{S}
.
If
x\in\mathcal{A}
, then
x\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
by rule

h-var
, and
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
by premise of the rule

NF-var
∙
.
If
x\in\mathcal{S}
, then
x\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by rule

s-var
.
Thus, we conclude with
x\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

2.

NF-lam
∙
.
Then,
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
By rule
h-lam
, we derive
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
so we conclude with

\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

3.

NF-app
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}{u\,s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-app${}^{\bullet}$}}

Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u\,s)}
implies in particular

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
, we can apply the
i.h.
on
t
, yielding

u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, as the positional flag is
@
.
Then,
u\,s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by rule

s-app
, which implies

u\,s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

4.

NF-esA
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esA${}^{\bullet}$}}

We assume
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
by
\alpha
-conversion,
so that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We have two possible cases, depending on whether

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
or
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Moreover, if
\mu=@
, then the latter is the only
possible case.

If
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
, then

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
by rule
h-sub
2
.
If
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by rule
s-sub
1
.

5.

NF-esS
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

∎

Remark B.5.

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
then
x\in\mathsf{fv}(t)
.

Lemma B.6.

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
.
If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
then
x\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
The interesting case is the
sub
∙
rule.
Then,
x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}v
,
where
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\}
, so
x\in\mathcal{A}
trivially.

The remaining cases are straightforward by resorting to the
i.h.
.
For example, in the case of rule
esLA
∙
, we have:

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)})}{u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[y\backslash s]}\textup{{esLA${}^{\bullet}$}}

Note that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[y\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},u)}
, so

x\in\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\}
by the
i.h.
on
u
.
Furthermore, we assume
y\neq x
by
\alpha
-conversion, so

x\in\mathcal{A}
, as required.
∎

Lemma B.7.

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
.
If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
then for every value
w
there exists
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
The interesting case is the
sub
∙
rule.
Then,
x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v
,
so
x\in\mathcal{A}
.
Hence, taking any value
w
, we yield

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}w=t^{\prime\prime}
by rule
sub
∙
.

The remaining cases are straightforward by resorting to the
i.h.
.
For example, in the case of the
esLA
∙
rule, we have:

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)})}{u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[y\backslash s]}\textup{{esLA${}^{\bullet}$}}

Note that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},u)}
, so we can apply
the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding
u^{\prime\prime}
such that

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime\prime}
.
Moreover, we assume
y\notin\mathsf{fv}(w)
by
\alpha
-conversion,
so we can apply rule
esLA
∙
to conclude with

u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime\prime}[y\backslash s]=t^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Lemma B.8.

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},t)}
and
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
with
x\notin\mathcal{A}
.
If
t\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}
,
then
t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

Proof.

By definition, there exist
\rho
and
t^{\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
holds by the hypothesis.
There are two cases, depending on whether
x\in\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
or not:

•

If
x\in\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
,
then
\rho=\mathsf{sub}_{(y,v)}
.
We have two subcases, depending on whether
x=y
or not:

–

x=y
.
Since
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
u
is of the form

w\mathtt{L}
by
Remark

4.1
.
Applying
Lemma

B.7
,
we yield
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime\prime}
.
We can apply rule
lsv
∙
, yielding

t[x\backslash w\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}[x\backslash w]\mathtt{L}
.

–

x\neq y
.
Let
w
be a value such that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(w)
.
There exists
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime\prime}

by
Lemma

B.7
.
We can apply rule
esLA
∙
, yielding

t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime\prime}[x\backslash u]
.

•

If
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
, we can apply rule
esLA
∙
,
yielding
t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}[x\backslash u]
.

In either case, we conclude with

t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.
∎

Lemma B.9.

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},t)}
and
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
with
x\notin\mathcal{S}
.
If
t\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}
,
then
t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

Proof.

By definition, there exist
\rho
and
t^{\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
holds by the hypothesis.
There are two cases, depending on whether
x\in\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
or not:

•

If
x\in\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
, then
\rho=\mathsf{sub}_{(y,v)}
.
We have two subcases, depending on whether
x=y
or not:

–

x=y
.
This case is not possible, since
x=y\in\mathcal{A}
by

Lemma

B.6
, and at the same time

x\notin\mathcal{A}
by the hypothesis.

–

x\neq y
.
Let
w
be a value such that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(w)
.
There exists
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}t^{\prime\prime}

by
Lemma

B.7
.
We can apply rule
esLS
∙
, yielding
t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime\prime}[x\backslash u]
.

•

If
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
, we can apply rule
esLS
∙
,
yielding
t[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}[x\backslash u]
.

In either case, we conclude with

t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.
∎

Completeness states that an irreducible term

t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
is in normal form with respect to
the same parameters
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mu
; that is,

t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.
However, there is an exception to this statement, since the context
surrounding
t
has to be taken into account.
In particular, an irreducible abstraction is
not
considered to
be a normal form if it occurs in an applied position, because the
evaluation of the whole term (including the surrounding context) can
proceed by means of a
\mathsf{db}
-step.
An hereditary abstraction
t
must be either a term of the form

(\lambda x.\,t^{\prime})\mathtt{L}
, or a term which is reducible in an applied
position, such as
x[x\backslash{\tt I}]
.
Thus, an applied hereditary abstraction is
always
reducible.

The first part of the following proposition covers the case in which
an irreducible term is a normal form, while the second and third parts
cover the case of applied hereditary abstractions, which are not in
normal form even if they are irreducible.

Proposition B.10 (Completeness of Useful Normal Forms).

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
.

1.

If
t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

and
(t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}})
,
then
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

2.

If
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
then either
t\in\mathsf{Abs}
or
t\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
.

3.

If
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
t\,u\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
,
for any term
u
.

Proof.

Statement
3
is an immediate consequence of statement
2
,
since if
t\in\mathsf{Abs}
, then
t\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
-reduces
by rule
db
∙
.
On the other hand, if
t\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
, then

t\,u\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
by applying rule
appL
∙
.

Statements
1
and
2
are proved by simultaneous
induction on
t
.

1.

•

t=x
.
If
x\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, it must be the case that
x\in\mathcal{A}
.
Thus,
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
.
We can then apply rule
NF-var
∙
, yielding

x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

•

t=\lambda x.\,u
.
Since
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}

by hypothesis.
We derive
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
by rule
NF-lam
∙
.

•

t=u\,s
.
Note that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u\,s)}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.

We necessarily have
u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
, since
otherwise
u\,s
would be in
\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

by rule
appL
∙
.
By the
i.h.
(
1
) on
u
, we yield
(a)
u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
.

As a consequence,
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by

Lemma

B.4
.
And thus we necessarily have
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
,
since otherwise
u\,s
would be in

\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
by rule
appR
∙
.
Then, (b)
s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
by the
i.h.
(
1
)
on
s
.
Applying rule
NF-app
∙
with (a) and (b) as premises, we derive

u\,s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

•

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
We necessarily have
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
,
since otherwise
u[x\backslash s]
would be in

\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
by rule
esR
∙
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
We can apply the
i.h.
(
1
) on
s
, yielding

s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.

By
Lemma

B.4
, we have
that
s
is either in
\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
or in
\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We analyse both cases:

–

s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Then
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies
in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}
by
the contraposition of
Lemma

B.8
.

Moreover, if

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
holds,
it can be derived either by (1)
h-sub
1
, meaning that

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and thus
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}

by
Remark

4.1
, or it can be derived either by
(2)
h-sub
2
, meaning that
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
.
So in this case, by the hypothesis in (
1
), we have

\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
(
1
) on
u
, yielding

u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.
By applying rule
NF-esA
∙
we derive

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

–

s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Then
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}
by
the contraposition of
Lemma

B.9
.

Since
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
holds by the invariant, then

s\notin\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
by
Lemma

B.2
, as

s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
As a consequence, if
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
we necessarily have
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
by rule
h-sub
1
,
and not by rule
h-sub
2
.
So in this case, by the hypothesis in (
1
), we have

\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
(
1
) on
u
, yielding

u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}
.
By applying rule
NF-esS
∙
we derive

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.

2.

•

t=x
.
Then,
x\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
is derived only by rule
h-var
,
so
x\in\mathcal{A}
.
We can apply rule
sub
∙
, yielding

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}v
, so we are done
since
x\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
.

•

t=\lambda y.\,u
. Immediate since
t\in\mathsf{Abs}
by definition.

•

t=u\,s
.
This case is not possible since
u\,s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}

never holds.

•

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
There are two cases depending on the rule we use to derive

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
:

–

h-sub
1
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}}{u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}\textup{{h-sub${}_{1}$}}

If
s\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
, then applying rule

esR
∙
we yield

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
, for any

\mu
, in particular
\mu=@
.

Otherwise,
s\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
(
1
) on
s
,
yielding
s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
Then,
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by

Lemma

B.4
.
We reason by cases:

*

If
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
(
2
) on
u
, yielding
either
u\in\mathsf{Abs}
or
u\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}
.
If the former, we conclude with
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Abs}

by definition.
If the latter, then

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
by

Lemma

B.8
, and
we are done.

*

If
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
(
2
) on
u
, yielding
either
u\in\mathsf{Abs}
or
u\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},@}
.
If the former, we conclude with
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Abs}

by definition.
If the latter, then

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
by

Lemma

B.9
, and we
are done.

–

h-sub
2
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}\textup{{h-sub${}_{2}$}}

Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
.
By the
i.h.
(
2
) on
u
, we yield either

u\in\mathsf{Abs}
or
u\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}
.
If the former, we conclude with
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Abs}

by definition.
If the latter, we apply
Lemma

B.8
,
yielding
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}
, so
we are done.

∎

The following corollary combines soundness
(
Proposition

B.3
) and
completeness (
Proposition

B.10
) for a term
t
in top-level
position,
i.e.
, when the abstraction frame
\mathcal{A}
is empty, the
structure frame
\mathcal{S}
is the set of all free variables of
t
,
and the positional flag is
\not
​@
:

Corollary B.11 (Characterisation of useful normal forms).

t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\mathsf{fv}(t),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
iff
t\in\mathsf{Irred}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\mathsf{fv}(t),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.

An example of this result is the term
(x\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]
.
On one hand, the term is in
\mathsf{Red}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
, since
it cannot reduce using any reduction rule, and on the other hand it
belongs to the set of normal forms by the following derivation:

\frac{\frac{\frac{}{x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\{y\},\{x\},@}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\bullet}$}}\frac{}{y\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\{y\},\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-var${}^{\bullet}$}}}{x\,y\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\{y\},\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-app${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{}{{\tt I}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-lam${}^{\bullet}$}}\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{}{{\tt I}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\varnothing}}\textup{{h-lam}}}{(x\,y)[y\backslash{\tt I}]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\{x\},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}}\textup{{NF-esA${}^{\bullet}$}}

B.2.
Diamond Property

Definition B.12 (Expansion of abstraction and structure frames).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame.
We inductively define the
expansion
of
\mathcal{A}
under
\mathtt{L}
,
written
\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\Diamond}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash t]}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\cup\{x\}&\text{if }t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\\
\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Analogously, let
\mathcal{S}
be a structure frame.
We inductively define the
expansion
of
\mathcal{S}
under
\mathtt{L}
,
written
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\Diamond}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash t]}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\cup\{x\}&\text{if }t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\\
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Lemma B.13.

t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
if and only if

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.

Proof.

We prove both implications by induction on the length of
\mathtt{L}
.
In the two directions we skip the base case, as it is straightforward.

We first show
t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
implies

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.
Then, we are in the case where
\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]
.
The judgement
t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}

can be derived either by rule
s-sub
1
or by rule
s-sub
2
:

•

s-sub
1
.
Then,
t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
x\notin\mathcal{S}
.
We apply the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
, yielding

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}}
.
Since
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}

by definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}
, then

\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}}\subseteq\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}}

by
Remark

4.1
.
Therefore,
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.

•

s-sub
2
.
Then,
t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}
,

x\notin\mathcal{S}
, and
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We apply the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
, yielding

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\cup\{x\}}
.
Since
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\cup\{x\}
.
We then conclude with
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.

We now show
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}

implies
t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
And we are in the case where
\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]
, so

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}}
by hypothesis.
We have two cases, by definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}
.

•

If
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then

\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\cup\{x\}
, so

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}\cup\{x\}}
, and

t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}
by the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
.
Moreover,
x\notin\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}
by
\alpha
-conversion
so in particular,
x\notin\mathcal{S}
, as

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}
.
Applying rule
s-sub
2
, we yield

t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

If
u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then

\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}
,
so
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}}
.
Moreover,
x\notin\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L^{\prime}}}
by
\alpha
-conversion
so in particular,
x\notin\mathcal{S}
.
Then,
t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
.
Applying rule
s-sub
1
, we yield

t\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

∎

Lemma B.14.

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},t)}
, and
let
\mathcal{B}
be a set of variables disjoint from
\mathcal{A}
.
If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}

with
v\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}}

and
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
,
then
t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
Note that cases
appL
∙
and
appR
∙
are impossible since

t=u\,s
, which cannot be an element of

\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
by
Remark

4.1
.
We analyse the remaining cases.

•

sub
∙
.
Then

t=x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v=t^{\prime}
,
with
\mu=@
.
Since
v\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}}
by hypothesis,
then
v\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
by
Remark

4.1
,
as
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}
.

•

esR
∙
.
Then

\frac{s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s^{\prime}}{t=u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u[y\backslash s^{\prime}]=t^{\prime}}\textup{{esR${}^{\bullet}$}}

We consider two cases: whether

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
is
derived by rule
h-sub
1
or by rule
h-sub
2
.
For both cases we consider
y\notin\mathcal{B}
by
\alpha
-conversion.

–

h-sub
1
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}

and
