y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}
.
We have
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by
Remark

4.1
.
We apply rule
h-sub
1
, yielding

u[y\backslash s^{\prime}]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.

–

h-sub
2
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}}
,

y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}
,
and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
.
We have
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by
Remark

4.1
, so

s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by the
i.h.
on
s
.
We apply rule
h-sub
2
, yielding

u[y\backslash s^{\prime}]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.

•

esLA
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)})}{t=u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[y\backslash s]=t^{\prime}}\textup{{esLA${}^{\bullet}$}}

The judgement
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}

can be derived either by rule
h-sub
1
or by rule
h-sub
2
.
The former has
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
as premise,
and since
\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}
,
then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}}

by
Remark

4.1
.
And it is also the case that
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}

by premise of the rule
esLA
∙
, so in both cases we proceed as follows.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}

then in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},u)}
.
Moreover,
v\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by
Remark

4.1
, so we apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HA}_{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\})\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.
Moreover,
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by
Remark

4.1
, as

\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}
.
And we assume
y\notin\mathcal{B}
by
\alpha
-conversion,
so we apply rule
h-sub
2
, yielding

u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.

•

esLS
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}\cup\{y\},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)})}{t=u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[y\backslash s]=t^{\prime}}\textup{{esLS${}^{\bullet}$}}

We consider two cases depending on whether

u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}

is derived using rule
h-sub
1
or rule
h-sub
2
:

–

h-sub
1
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}

and
y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}

then in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}\cup\{y\},u)}
.
We apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.
Applying rule
h-sub
1
we obtain

u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}
.

–

h-sub
2
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}}
,

y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}
, and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
.
Moreover,
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by premise of the rule
esLS
∙
.
Then
(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\cap\mathcal{S}\neq\varnothing

by contraposition of
Lemma

B.2
,
which contradicts
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
.
Hence this case is impossible.

∎

Definition B.15 (Hereditary variables).

The set of
hereditary variables
under a variable
x
is
written
\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
and is defined inductively as follows:

\frac{}{x\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}}\textup{{hv-var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}\hskip 8.5359ptx\neq y}{t[y\backslash u]\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}}\textup{{hv-sub${}_{1}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{HVar}_{y}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}}{t[y\backslash u]\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}}\textup{{hv-sub${}_{2}$}}

Remark B.16.

If
t\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
and
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\mathtt{L})
,
then
t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
.

Lemma B.17.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame and let
x
be any variable.
If
t\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
, then
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by induction on the derivation of the
judgement
t\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
.
∎

Lemma B.18.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame, and let

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
be an arbitrary partition.
If
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then either
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}

or there exists
x\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
t\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}}
.

•

h-var
.
Then
t=y
,
and
y\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}

by premise of the rule
h-var
.
There are two cases, depending on whether

y\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
or
y\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
If
y\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
, then
y\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}

by rule
h-var
.
If
y\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
, then
y\in\mathsf{HVar}_{y}

by rule
hv-var
.

•

h-lam
.
Then
t=\lambda y.\,u
.
We apply rule
h-lam
,
yielding
\lambda y.\,u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.

•

h-sub
1
.
Then
t=u[y\backslash s]
.
The judgement

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}}

is derived from
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}}

and
y\notin\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
We take
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup(\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\{y\})
and
apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding two possible cases:

–

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.
Since
y\notin\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
we can apply rule
h-sub
1
, yielding

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.

–

u\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
, for some
x\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Since
y\notin\mathcal{A}_{2}
, then
x\neq y

and we can apply rule
hv-sub
1
,
yielding
u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HVar}_{y}
.

•

h-sub
2
.
Then
t=u[y\backslash s]
.
The judgement

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}}

is derived from

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{(\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2})\cup\{y\}}
,

y\notin\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}}
.
By the
i.h.
on
u
we have two possible cases:

–

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.
Since
y\notin\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
we can apply rule
h-sub
1
, yielding

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.

–

u\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}
for some

x\in(\mathcal{A}_{2}\cup\{y\})
.
We have two cases, depending on whether
x=y
or not:

*

If
x=y
, then by the
i.h.
on
s

we have two possible subcases:

·

s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}
.
Since

y\notin\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
and
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\{y\}}

by
Lemma

B.17
,
then
u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}

by rule
h-sub
2
.

·

s\in\mathsf{HVar}_{z}
for some
z\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
Then

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HVar}_{z}

by rule
hv-sub
2
.

*

If
x\neq y
, then

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HVar}_{x}

by rule
hv-sub
1
.

∎

Lemma B.19.

t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
if and only if

t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward by induction, analogous to
Lemma

B.13
.
∎

Lemma B.20 (Hereditary abstractions and structures are closed by reduction).

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
.

1.

Let
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}

and
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}

where
\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}
.
Then
t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

If
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}

and
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
then
t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.
∎

Given a substitution context
\mathtt{L}
, we can use the reduction rules
defined in
Section

4
to define the notion of
useful
reduction for substitution contexts
, by just understanding these
elements as terms, where
\Diamond
is taken as a free variable.

Remark B.21.

1.

If
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}u

and
\Diamond\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
,
then
u
is of the form
(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
,
and
\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Diamond\},\mu}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

2.

If
v\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}t

and
\Diamond\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
,
then there exists
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
such that
t=v\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
,
and
\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Diamond\},\mu}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

3.

If
\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{\Diamond\},\mu}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
,
then
t\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

Lemma B.22.

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
then for all sets
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

such that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

it holds that
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.

•

db
∙
.
Then
t=(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}
,
where
\rho=\mathsf{db}
.
By rule
db
∙
, we conclude

(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}u[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}
.

•

sub
∙
.
Then

t=x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v=t^{\prime}
,
where
\rho=\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}
and
\mu=@
.
By rule
sub
∙
, we conclude

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},@}v
.

•

lsv
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptv\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t=u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}}\textup{{lsv${}^{\bullet}$}}

where
\rho=\mathsf{lsv}
.
If
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then in
particular
\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\}
.
Therefore,

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}u^{\prime}

by the
i.h.
on
u
.
Moreover,
v\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
by
Remark

4.1
, and
we can always assume
x\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Applying rule
lsv
∙
, we conclude

u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
.

•

appL
∙
.
Straightforward by the
i.h.
.

•

appR
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359pts\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s^{\prime}}{t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u\,s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}}\textup{{appR${}^{\bullet}$}}

We have
s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s^{\prime}

by the
i.h.
on
s
.
Moreover,
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
by
Remark

4.1
, so
applying rule
appR
∙
, we conclude with

u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}u\,s^{\prime}
.

•

esR
∙
.
Straightforward by the
i.h.
.

•

esLA
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{t=u[x\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]=t^{\prime}}\textup{{esLA${}^{\bullet}$}}

As
\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\}
, we can
then apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}u^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
by
Remark

4.1
.
We assume
x\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
also, by
\alpha
-conversion.
Applying rule
esLA
∙
, we conclude with

u[x\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]
.

•

esLS
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

∎

Proposition B.23 (Towards the Diamond Property).

Let
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
and suppose that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t_{1}
and

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t_{2}
, with
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
.
Assume that if
\rho_{1}=\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{1})}
and

\rho_{2}=\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{2})}
, then
v_{1}=v_{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{B}^{2}
be sets of variables, disjoint from
\mathcal{A}
,
such that if
\rho_{i}=\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{i})}
, then

v_{i}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{i}}
, for all
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then, there exists a term
t^{\prime}
such that

t_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
and

t_{2}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.
Case
t=\lambda x.\,u
is impossible, as there are no rules for
reducing abstractions.

1.

t=x
.
This case does not apply since we would have

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{1})},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v_{1}=t_{1}
and

x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v_{2})},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v_{2}=t_{2}
, with
v_{1}=v_{2}=v
by the hypothesis
but which contradicts the hypothesis stating
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
.

2.

t=u\,s
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u\,s)}
, then

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
hold.
Applications reduce by rules
db
∙
,
appL
∙
, and
appR
∙
,
so we consider the following subcases:

2.1

db
∙
-
db
∙
.
This case does not apply since it ends up being that

t_{1}=t_{2}
, which contradicts the hypothesis.

2.2

db
∙
-
appL
∙
.
Then,

t=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}=t_{1}
, where

u=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}
and
\rho_{1}=\mathsf{db}
.
On the other hand,

t=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{2}\,s=t_{2}

is derived from

(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}u_{2}
,
where
u_{2}
is of the form
(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}^{\prime}

by
Remark

B.21
.
Then,
t_{2}=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
by rule
db
∙
, and

t_{1}=u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
by

Remark

B.21
.
And we conclude by applying
Lemma

B.22

on both reduction steps to extend
\mathcal{A}
to

\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1}
and
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2}
, respectively.
The following diagram summarises the proof:

\textstyle{t=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\,s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}=t_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{t_{2}=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\,s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=t^{\prime}}

2.3

db
∙
-
appR
∙
.
Then,

t=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}=t_{1}
,
where

u=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}
and
\rho_{1}=\mathsf{db}
.
On the other hand,

t=(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\,s_{2}=t_{2}
is derived from

(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and

s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s_{2}
.
This case is impossible since

(\lambda x.\,u^{\prime})\mathtt{L}
cannot be an element of
\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}

by
Remark

4.1
.

2.4

appL
∙
-
appR
∙
.
Then,

t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}\,s=t_{1}

derived from
(1)
u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}u_{1}
.
On the other hand,

t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u\,s_{2}=t_{2}
,
derived from (2)
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
(3)
s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s_{2}
.
We can apply rule
appL
∙
with (1) as premise, yielding

t_{2}=u\,s_{2}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}\,s_{2}=t^{\prime}
.
Having (1) and (2), then
u_{1}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by

Lemma

B.20
.
With this result and (3) we can apply rule
appR
∙
, yielding

t_{1}=u_{1}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}\,s_{2}=t^{\prime}
.
And we conclude by applying
Lemma

B.22

on both reductions to extend
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1}

and
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2}
, respectively.
The following diagram summarises the proof:

\textstyle{t=u\,s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}

\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{t_{2}=u\,s_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{u_{1}\,s_{2}=t^{\prime}}

2.5

appL
∙
-
appL
∙
.
Then,

t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}\,s=t_{1}

derived from
u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}u_{1}
.
On the other hand,

t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{2}\,s=t_{2}
,
derived from

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}u_{2}
,
where

since

by hypothesis.
We apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

such that

u_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{S},@}u^{\prime}

and

u_{2}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2},\mathcal{S},@}u^{\prime}
.
Applying rule
appL
∙
to reduce both
u_{1}\,s
and

u_{2}\,s
, we obtain

t_{1}=u_{1}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\,s=t^{\prime}

and

t_{2}=u_{2}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\,s=t^{\prime}
,
respectively.
The following diagram summarises the proof:

\textstyle{t=u\,s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{u_{1}\,s=t_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{2},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{1},\mathcal{S},\mu}

\scriptstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{1},\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}^{2},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{u^{\prime}\,s=t^{\prime}}

2.6

appR
∙
-
appR
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

3.

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
, then

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
,

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
, and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
Closures reduce by rules
lsv
∙
,
esR
∙
,
esLA
∙
, and

∙
, so we consider the following cases:

3.1

lsv
∙
-
lsv
∙
.
Then

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359ptv\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t=u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}=t_{1}}\textup{{lsv${}^{\bullet}$}}

where
s=v\mathtt{L}
and
\rho_{1}=\mathsf{lsv}

\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
.
We start first by discussing in

C.1
the
partial unfolding operation we introduced in
Section

5
:
we give in particular a characterisation of the partial unfolding
operation via rewriting.
Our main goal here is to show that the relation
\to_{\sigma}
is
terminating (
Theorem

C.14
).
For achieving this result, we prove that there is a decreasing measure,
which we define in this same subsection.
Then, we move on to
Section

C.2
, where we show
the technical results that are necessary to relate
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
(
Corollary

C.26
).

C.1.
Characterising the Unfolding Operation via Rewriting

The reduction relation under a value assignment
\sigma
, written

\to_{\sigma}
, is defined as follows:

\to_{\sigma}\,:=\,\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}\cup_{x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)}(\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,\sigma(x))}})

A term
t
is said to be
\sigma
-reducible
if there exists

u
such that
t\to_{\sigma}u
.
Note that
\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
plays the role of a value frame, so this notion
is closely related to the set
\mathsf{Red}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
(defined in
Appendix

A
).

We start by proving that
\to_{\sigma}
is
terminating
(
cf.

Section

2
).
To prove this, we define a
measure

\#^{\sigma}(\_)
on terms
and we show that it is strictly decreasing w.r.t.
\to_{\sigma}

(
Lemma

C.7
).
The measure
\#^{\sigma}(\_)
is inspired by de Vrijer’s direct proof
of the finite developments theorem
[
de Vrijer, 1985
]
, and defined by
means of intermediate functions
\#_{x}(\_)
and
\#(\_)
.

Given a term
t
and a variable
x
, the

potential number of occurences of
x
in
t
, written

\#_{x}(t)
, is defined as 0 if
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
, and
otherwise is defined recursively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{1cm}}rcl}\#_{x}(x)&:=&1\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\#_{x}(t\,u)&:=&\#_{x}(t)+\#_{x}(u)\\
\#_{x}(\lambda y.\,t)&:=&0\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\#_{x}(t[y\backslash u])&:=&\#_{x}(t)+\#_{x}(u)\cdot(1+\#_{y}(t))\end{array}

This gives an
overapproximation
of the number of free reachable
occurrences of
x
in the unfolding of
t
.
By this we mean that
\#_{x}(t)
counts the
potential
number
of free occurrences of
x
in the unfolding of
t
,
even if not all the substitutions are
performed during useful reduction for some reason.
For example,
\#_{y}(x[x\backslash y\,y])=4
, although the substitution of
x
by
y\,y

is never executed because it is not useful.
Another example is
t_{0}:=x[x\backslash y[y\backslash z]]
.
We have
\#_{z}(t_{0})=4
, despite the fact that
z

only occurs three times in the corresponding unfolding

z[x\backslash z][y\backslash z]
.

We now define the
measure of
t
, written
\#(t)
, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace{1cm}}rcl}\#(x)&:=&0\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\#(t\,u)&:=&\#(t)+\#(u)\\
\#(\lambda x.\,t)&:=&0\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\#(t[x\backslash u])&:=&\#(t)+\#_{x}(t)+\#(u)\cdot(1+\#_{x}(t))\end{array}

This measure can be seen as the number of
steps that the evaluation takes to perform all the
substitutions in the longest reduction sequence starting at
t
.
Notice that
\#(v)=0
for any value
v
.

Furthermore, given
\sigma
a value assignment,
we define the
measure of
t
under
\sigma
, written
\#^{\sigma}(t)
, as

\#^{\sigma}(t):=\#(t)+\textstyle\sum_{x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)}\#_{x}(t)
.

For example, take again the term
t_{0}
from above.
Since
\to_{\sigma}
-reduction is non-deterministic,
we consider two different reduction sequences from
t_{0}
,
of lengths
2
and
3
respectively,
where the second sequence is the longest possible.
In this example,
\sigma
is the empty value assignment
(\sigma=\cdot)
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l@{\,\,}l}t_{0}=x[x\backslash y[y\backslash z]]&\to&u=x[x\backslash z[y\backslash z]]&\to&t_{3}=z[x\backslash z][y\backslash z]\\
t_{0}=x[x\backslash y[y\backslash z]]&\to&t_{1}=y[x\backslash y][y\backslash z]&\to&t_{2}=y[x\backslash z][y\backslash z]\\
&&&\to&t_{3}=z[x\backslash z][y\backslash z]\end{array}

Then

\#_{z}(t_{0})=4
,
\#_{z}(t_{1})=3
,

\#_{z}(t_{2})=3
,
\#_{z}(t_{3})=3
,
and
\#_{z}(u)=4
.
Note for example that
\#_{z}(t_{0})=4

is greater than the actual number of free reachable occurrences
of
z
in its unfolding (which is
3
).

Note also that
\#(t_{0})=3
,

\#(t_{1})=2
,

\#(t_{2})=1
,
\#(t_{3})=0
,
and
\#(u)=1
.
These are upper bounds for the number of substitution steps required to
completely unfold these terms.
For instance,
\#(t_{0})=3
, even though the first reduction sequence
reaches the unfolding in only two steps.
The important point is that the measure strictly decreases at each step
of any reduction sequence, as we prove at the end of this subsection.

Given a value frame
\mathcal{V}
,
the set of
substitution contexts in normal form under
\mathcal{V}

is written
\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
and is defined inductively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 21.63199pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=49.9029pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{Ctx-NF-empty}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Diamond\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{Val}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=154.06512pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{Ctx-NF-addVal}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 40.62633pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pt\neg(t\in\mathsf{Val})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=150.25258pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{Ctx-NF-addNonVal}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 38.72005pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}$}}}}\end{array}

Definition C.1 (Expansion of value frames).

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a value frame.
We inductively define the
expansion of
\mathcal{V}
under
\mathtt{L}
,
written
\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{L}}
, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{V}^{\Diamond}&:=&\mathcal{V}\\
\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash t]}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}\cup\{x\}&\text{if }t\in\mathsf{Val}\\
\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Lemma C.2.

The following are equivalent:

1.

t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V},\mu}

2.

t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}^{\mathtt{L}},\mu}

and
\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

Simultaneously by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.
∎

Definition C.3.

Let
\varphi:\mathsf{Var}\to\mathbb{N}
.
Given a substitution context
\mathtt{L}
, the

potential number of occurrences of
x
in
\mathtt{L}
under
\varphi
,
written
\#^{\varphi}_{x}(\mathtt{L})
, is recursively defined as follows:

\#^{\varphi}_{x}(\Diamond):=\varphi(x)\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\#^{\varphi}_{x}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[y\backslash t]):=\#^{\varphi}_{x}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime})+\#_{x}(t)\cdot(1+\#^{\varphi}_{y}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime}))

We define the
measure of
\mathtt{L}
under
\varphi
,
written
\#^{\varphi}(\mathtt{L})
, as follows:

\#^{\varphi}(\Diamond):=0\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\hskip 8.5359pt\#^{\varphi}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash t]):=\#^{\varphi}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime})+\#^{\varphi}_{x}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime})+\#(t)\cdot(1+\#^{\varphi}_{x}(\mathtt{L}^{\prime}))

For any term
t
, we define
\varphi_{t}(x):=\#_{x}(t)
.

Lemma C.4 (Splitting of measures).

Let
t
be a term and
\mathtt{L}
be a substitution context.
Then:

1.

\#_{x}(t\mathtt{L})=\#^{\varphi_{t}}_{x}(\mathtt{L})

2.

\#(t\mathtt{L})=\#(t)+\#^{\varphi_{t}}(\mathtt{L})

Proof.

Simultaneously by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.
∎

Lemma C.5.

Let
t
and
u
be terms and
\mathtt{L}
be a substitution context.
If
\mathsf{fv}(t)\mathrel{\#}\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{L})
, then:

1.

\#^{\varphi_{t[y\backslash u]}}_{x}(\mathtt{L})\leq\#_{x}(t)+\#^{\varphi_{u}}_{x}(\mathtt{L})\cdot(1+\#_{y}(t))

for any variable
x

2.

\#^{\varphi_{t[x\backslash u]}}(\mathtt{L})\leq\#^{\varphi_{u}}(\mathtt{L})\cdot(1+\#_{x}(t))

Proof.

Simultaneously by induction on
\mathtt{L}
.
∎

Lemma C.6.

Let
t
be a term such that
t\to_{\sigma}t^{\prime}
for some term
t^{\prime}
,
and let
x
be a variable such that if
t

(\mathsf{sub}_{(y,v)})
-reduces to
t^{\prime}
, then
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(v)
.
Then,
\#_{x}(t)\geq\#_{x}(t^{\prime})
.
Moreover, if
x=y
, then
\#_{x}(t)>\#_{x}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the judgement
t\to_{\sigma}t^{\prime}
.
∎

Now we can state a lemma that involves the general notion of decreasing measure:

Lemma C.7.

Let
t
be a term. Then:

•

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}
, then
\#(t)\geq\#(t^{\prime})
when

\rho=\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}
or
\#(t)>\#(t^{\prime})
when

\rho=\mathsf{lsv}
.

•

If
t\to_{\sigma}t^{\prime}
, then
\#^{\sigma}(t)>\#^{\sigma}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The proof uses

Lemmas

C.8
,
C.9
and
C.10
.
∎

Lemma C.8.

Let
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t^{\prime}
.
Then,
\#(t)\geq\#(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the judgement

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma C.9.

Let
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}t^{\prime}
.
Then,
\#(t)>\#(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the judgement
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}t^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma C.10.

Let
t\to_{\sigma}t^{\prime}
.
Then,
\#^{\sigma}(t)>\#^{\sigma}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By case analysis on the rule used to derive
t\to_{\sigma}t^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma C.11.

Let
v
be a value and
\mathtt{L}
a substitution context. Then:

1.

If there exists
t
such that
v\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t
,
then there exist
v^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
such that
t=v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

2.

If
v\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
, then

t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
,
for any term
t
.

Proof.

We prove each item independently.

1.

By induction on the derivation of
v\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t
.
Note that it is not possible to apply rules
db
∘
,
appL
∘
nor
appR
∘
, given that
v\mathtt{L}
is not an application.
Then, we are left to analyse the following cases:

•

sub
∘
.
Then,
x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)}}w
, where

v=x
,
\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
, and
t=w
,
so we are done, with
v^{\prime}=w
and
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\Diamond
.

•

lsv
∘
.
Then,
\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash w\mathtt{L}_{2}]
, and thus

\frac{v\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,w)}}t_{1}}{v\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash w\mathtt{L}_{2}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}t_{1}[x\backslash w]\mathtt{L}_{2}=t}\textup{{lsv${}^{\circ}$}}

We apply the
i.h.
on
v\mathtt{L}_{1}
, yielding
v^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}

such that
t_{1}=v^{\prime}_{1}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}
.
And we are done, with
v^{\prime}=v^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}[x\backslash w]\mathtt{L}_{2}
.

•

esL
∘
.
Then,
\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash u]
, and thus

\frac{v\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t_{1}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{v\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t_{1}[x\backslash u]}\textup{{esL${}^{\circ}$}}

We apply the
i.h.
on
v\mathtt{L}_{1}
, yielding
v^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}

such that
t_{1}=v^{\prime}_{1}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}
.
And we are done, with
v^{\prime}=v^{\prime}_{1}
and

\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}_{1}[x\backslash u]
.

•

esR
∘
.
Then,
\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash u]
, thus deriving

v\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash u^{\prime}]

from

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}u^{\prime}
.
We are done with
v^{\prime}=v
and
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\mathtt{L}_{1}[x\backslash u^{\prime}]
,

2.

By induction on the length of
\mathtt{L}
.

•

\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
.
Then,
v\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v^{\prime}
, which can only be derived by
rule
sub
∘
.
We obtain
t[x\backslash v]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]

by applying rule
esR
∘
.

•

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]
.
We analyse different cases, depending on the rule used to derive

v\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.
Since it is not possible to apply rules
sub
∘
,
db
∘
,

appL
∘
, and
appR
∘
, given that

v\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]
is neither a variable nor an
application, so we are left to analyse the following three cases:

–

lsv
∘
.
Then
u=w\mathtt{L}_{2}
and
\rho=\mathsf{lsv}
, deriving

\frac{v\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)}}s}{v\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash w\mathtt{L}_{2}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}s[y\backslash w]\mathtt{L}_{2}}\textup{{lsv${}^{\circ}$}}

Moreover,
s=v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}_{3}
by point (1), hence we
necessarily have
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\mathtt{L}_{3}[y\backslash w]\mathtt{L}_{2}
.
Thus in particular
v\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}_{3}
.
We apply the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L}_{1}
, yielding

t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)}}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}_{3}
.
Applying rule
lsv
∘
, we obtain

t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}=t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash w\mathtt{L}_{2}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)}}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}_{3}[y\backslash w]\mathtt{L}_{2}=t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

–

esL
∘
.
Then

\frac{v\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}s\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{v\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}s[y\backslash u]}\textup{{esL${}^{\circ}$}}

Moreover,
s=v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}_{3}
by point (1), hence we
necessarily have
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\mathtt{L}_{3}[y\backslash u]
.
Thus in particular
v\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}_{3}
.
We apply the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L}_{1}
, yielding

t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}_{3}
.
Given that
y\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
, we can then apply
rule
esL
∘
, yielding

t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}=t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}_{3}[y\backslash u]=t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

–

esR
∘
.
Then,

v\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u^{\prime}]

is derived from

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}u^{\prime}
.
Hence,
v^{\prime}=v
and
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}=\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u^{\prime}]
.
By applying
esR
∘
, we obtain

t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}=t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}_{1}[y\backslash u^{\prime}]=t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
.

∎

Lemma C.12.

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t_{1}
and

v\mathtt{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v^{\prime}\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
, then there exists
t^{\prime}_{1}

such that
t_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}^{=}_{\rho}t^{\prime}_{1}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t_{1}
.
The most interesting cases are
sub
∘
and
esL
∘
, while the
rest are analogous to the
esL
∘
case.

•

sub
∘
.
Then,
t=x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}v=t_{1}
, so we take

t^{\prime}_{1}:=v
, so we are done.

•

esL
∘
.
Then

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}u_{1}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)})}{t=u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}u_{1}[y\backslash s]=t_{1}}\textup{{esL${}^{\circ}$}}

We apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding
u^{\prime}_{1}
such that

u_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}^{=}_{\rho}u^{\prime}_{1}
.
We may assume
y\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
by
\alpha
-conversion,
so we can apply rule
esL
∘
, yielding

t_{1}=u_{1}[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}^{=}_{\rho}u^{\prime}_{1}[y\backslash s]=t^{\prime}_{1}
.

∎

Proposition C.13 (Local Confluence / WCR of
\to_{\sigma}
).

Let
t
be a
\sigma
-reducible term such that

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{1}}t_{1}
and

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{2}}t_{2}
, with

\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\to_{\sigma}
.
Then, there exists
t^{\prime}
such that
t_{1}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}^{*}_{\rho_{2}}t^{\prime}

and
t_{2}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}^{*}_{\rho_{1}}t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.
Case
t=\lambda x.\,u
is impossible, since there are no rules
to reduce abstractions. We analyse the remaining cases.

•

t=x
.
The only rule to reduce
x
is
sub
∘
, and thus

t=x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,\mathsf{dom}(\sigma))}}\sigma(x)=t_{1}=t_{2}
.
So we are done.

•

t=u\,s
.
We can
\sigma
-reduce
t
with the rules
appL
∘
and
appR
∘
,
so we have the following cases:

–

appL
∘
-
appR
∘
.
Then,
t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{1}}u_{1}\,s=t_{1}
, which is derived from
(1)
u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{1}}u_{1}
, and we have

t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{2}}u\,s_{2}=t_{2}
, which is derived from
(2)
s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{2}}s_{2}
.
We apply rule
appL
∘
with (1) as premise, yielding

t_{2}=u\,s_{2}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{1}}u_{1}\,s_{2}=t^{\prime}
.
And we apply rule
appR
∘
with (2) as premise, yielding

t_{1}=u_{1}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho_{2}}u_{1}\,s_{2}=t^{\prime}
.
