Note that the second point of
Lemma

C.7
provides
a
decreasing measure
for
\to_{\sigma}
reduction, and the
previous lemma states that
\to_{\sigma}
is locally confluent.
Hence:

Theorem C.14.

The reduction relation
\to_{\sigma}
is terminating and confluent.
In particular, a term
t
always has a unique
\to_{\sigma}
-normal form.

Proof.

Termination is a straightforward consequence of

Lemma

C.7
.
Confluence results from the local confluence of
\to_{\sigma}

(
Proposition

C.13
in
Appendix

C
) and
Newman’s Lemma (
cf.

Section

2
).
Confluence ensures the uniqueness of normal forms.
∎

Notably, from this theorem we conclude that

Corollary C.15.

The reduction relation
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}
is terminating.

Let
\sigma
be a value assignment. The following corollary relates
the normal form of the reduction relation
\to_{\sigma}
and the partial
unfolding of a term under
\sigma
.

Corollary C.16.

For any term
t
and any value assigment
\sigma
, the (unique)

\sigma
-normal form of
t
is
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

C.14
,
\to_{\sigma}
is terminating, so
there exists a term
u
such that
t\to^{*}_{\sigma}u
and

u
is in
\sigma
-normal form.
By
Lemma

C.18
(
2
)
(see
Appendix

C
) we have
t\to^{*}_{\sigma}{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
Moreover, by
Lemma

C.18
(
1
)

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is in
\sigma
-normal form.
Therefore by confluence of
\to_{\sigma}
(
Theorem

C.14
),
we conclude that
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}=u
.
∎

In particular, in the top-level case,
{t}^{\downarrow}

is the (unique)
\mathsf{lsv}
-normal form of
t
.

C.2.
Relating
\textup{{lcbv}}^{\circ}
and
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}

Lemma C.17.

Let
v\mathtt{L}
be a term in
\sigma
-normal form and
t
be a term
in
(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))
-normal form.
Then,
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
is in
\sigma
-normal form.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathtt{L}
.

•

\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
.
There are three rules to reduce
t[x\backslash v]
.
We argue that none of them apply.

1.

If
t[x\backslash v]
reduces by rule
lsv
∘
, then

\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)}}t^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash v]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}t^{\prime}[x\backslash v]}\textup{{lsv${}^{\circ}$}}

but we reach a contradiction since
t
is in

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))
-normal form.

2.

If
t[x\backslash v]
reduces by rule
esL
∘
, given a rule
name
\rho
such that
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}\in\to_{\sigma}
, we have

\frac{t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{t[x\backslash v]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t^{\prime}[x\backslash v]}\textup{{esL${}^{\circ}$}}

but we reach a contradiction, since
t
is in

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))
-normal form.

3.

If
t[x\backslash v]
reduces by rule
esR
∘
, given a rule
name
\rho
such that
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}\in\to_{\sigma}
, we have

\frac{v\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}v^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash v]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v^{\prime}]}\textup{{esR${}^{\circ}$}}

but we reach a contradiction since
v
is a value, and so it
is in
\sigma
-normal form.

•

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u]
.
There are three rules to reduce
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u]
.
We argue that none of them apply.

1.

If
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u]
reduces by rule

lsv
∘
, then
u=w\mathtt{L}_{1}
and

\frac{t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(y,w)}}r}{t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash w\mathtt{L}_{1}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{lsv}}r[y\backslash w]\mathtt{L}_{1}}\textup{{lsv${}^{\circ}$}}

Since
v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash w\mathtt{L}_{1}]
is in

\sigma
-normal form, then
v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
is in

(\sigma\cup(y\mapsto w))
-normal form.
By
\alpha
-conversion,
y\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
, so we also
have that
t
is in

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)\cup(y\mapsto w))
-normal form.
Then,
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
is in

(\sigma\cup(y\mapsto w))
-normal form by the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
, so we reach a contradiction.
Hence
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
is in
\sigma
-normal form.

2.

If
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u]
reduces by rule

esL
∘
, given a rule name
\rho
such that

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}\in\to_{\sigma}
, we have

\frac{t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}r\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)}{t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[x\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}r[x\backslash u]}\textup{{esL${}^{\circ}$}}

since
v\mathtt{L}
is in
\sigma
-normal form, then

v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
is in
(\sigma\cup(y\mapsto w))
-normal form.
By
\alpha
-conversion,
y\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
, so we also have
that
t
is in

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)\cup(y\mapsto w))
-normal form.
Then,
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
is in

(\sigma\cup(y\mapsto w))
-normal form by the
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
, so we reach a contradiction.
Hence
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
is in
\sigma
-normal form.

3.

If
t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u]
reduces by rule

esR
∘
, given a rule name
\rho
such that

\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}\in\to_{\sigma}
, we have

\frac{u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}u^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho}t[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L^{\prime}}[y\backslash u^{\prime}]}\textup{{esR${}^{\circ}$}}

but we reach a contradiction since
v\mathtt{L}
is in

\sigma
-normal form by hypothesis.

∎

Lemma C.18.

Let
t
be a term and
\sigma
a value assignment. Then:

1.

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is in
\sigma
-normal form

2.

t\to^{*}_{\sigma}{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}

Proof.

We prove each item separately, and on both we proceed by induction on
t
.

1.

•

t=x
.
There are two cases, depending on the form of
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}
:

–

If
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, then
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=\sigma(x)
.
If
\sigma(x)
is a variable, then
\sigma(x)
is in

\sigma
-normal form as there are no reduction rules in

\to_{\sigma}
to reduce
\sigma(x)
:
\sigma(x)\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
because
\sigma
is idempotent.
If
\sigma(x)
is an abstraction, then
\sigma(x)
is in

\sigma
-normal form as there are no reduction rules to reduce
abstractions.

–

Otherwise,
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
and
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=x
.
We conclude
x
is in
\sigma
-normal form
since there are no reduction rules in
\to_{\sigma}
to reduce
x
.

•

t=\lambda x.\,u
.
Then,
{(\lambda x.\,u)}^{\downarrow\sigma}=\lambda x.\,u
, which is in

\sigma
-normal form since there are no reduction rules in

\to_{\sigma}
to reduce an abstraction.

•

t=u\,s
.
Then,
{(u\,s)}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
There are two rules that allow us to reduce

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
. We argue that neither applies:

–

We can apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is
in
\sigma
-normal form, so
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}

does not reduce by rule
appL
∘
.

–

We can apply the
i.h.
on
s
, yielding
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}

is in
\sigma
-normal form, so

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
does not reduce by rule

appR
∘
.

•

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
There are two cases, depending on the form of

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}
:

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
and
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
,
then
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
,
which is in
\sigma
-normal form by the
i.h.
and by

Lemma

C.17
.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
Let us reason by contradiction, assuming that the term is
\sigma
-reducible.
Firstly,
if
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]

reduces by rule
esR
∘
,
then it is also the case that
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is
\sigma
-reducible,
therefore we reach a contradiction
since
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is in
\sigma
-normal form
by
i.h.
on
s
.
Then the only way to reduce the whole term is by first reducing

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
,
but we reach again a contradiction,
since
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is in
\sigma
-normal form
by
i.h.
on
u
.
Then
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]

is in
\sigma
-normal form.

2.

•

t=x
.
There are two cases depending on the form of
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}
:

–

If
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, then
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=\sigma(x)
.
We conclude
x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,\sigma(x))}}\sigma(x)

by rule
sub
∘
.

–

Otherwise,
x\to^{*}_{\sigma}x={x}^{\downarrow\sigma}
in zero steps,
so we are done.

•

t=\lambda x.\,u
.
Then,

\lambda x.\,u\to^{*}_{\sigma}\lambda x.\,u={(\lambda x.\,u)}^{\downarrow\sigma}

in zero steps, so we are done.

•

t=u\,s
.
Then
{(u\,s)}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
We can apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
Hence, we have the reduction sequence

u\,s\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,s
in
which each step is obtained by applying rule
appL
∘
.
Analogously, we can apply the
i.h.
on
s
, yielding

s\to^{*}_{\sigma}{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
Hence we have the reduction sequence

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,s\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}

in which each step is obtained
by applying rule
appR
∘
.
Then we can conclude
u\,s\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.

•

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
There are two cases depending on the form of
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}
:

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
and
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
,
then
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
.
We can apply
i.h.
on
s
, yielding

s\to^{*}_{\sigma}{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
Hence we have the reduction sequence

u[x\backslash s]\to^{*}_{\sigma}u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]

in which each step is obtained by applying rule
esR
∘
.
On the other hand, we can apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u\to^{*}_{\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}
;
we can then write this reduction sequence as

u\to^{*}_{\sigma}u^{\prime}\to_{\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}
,
for some
u^{\prime}
.
Then

u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\to^{*}_{\sigma}u^{\prime}[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]

by rule
esL
∘
, and

u^{\prime}[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\to_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}

by rule
lsv
∘
.
Then we can conclude

u[x\backslash s]\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
We can apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
Hence, we have the reduction sequence

u[x\backslash s]\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash s]

in which each step is obtained by applying rule
esL
∘
, since

x
does not occur free in the rule name by
\alpha
-conversion.
On the other hand, we can apply the
i.h.
on
s
, yielding

s\to^{*}_{\sigma}{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
Hence, we have the reduction sequence

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash s]\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
in which each step
is obtained by applying rule
esR
∘
.
Therefore, we can conclude

u[x\backslash s]\to^{*}_{\sigma}{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.

∎

Remark C.19.

If
t\in\mathsf{Val}
, then
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Val}
.

Remark C.20.

If
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
, then
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Abs}
,
with
\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})
.

Lemma C.21.

Let
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
be value assignments and
t
be a term.
Then:

1.

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\in\mathsf{Val}

if and only if

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val}
.

2.

Let
v
be a value.
If
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and (
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}
for all
x\in\mathcal{S}
), then there
exists
\mathtt{L}_{1}
such that
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{1}

if and only if there exists
\mathtt{L}_{2}
such that

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v\mathtt{L}_{2}
.

Proof.

We prove the two statements simultaneously.

1.

We reason by induction on
t
, showing only the left-to-right
implication since the other is similar.

•

t=x
.
There are two cases, depending on the form of
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}
.
If
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=\sigma_{2}(x)
, then we conclude

\sigma_{2}(x)\in\mathsf{Val}
by definition.
Otherwise,
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=x
, and we conclude since

x\in\mathsf{Val}
.

•

t=\lambda x.\,u
.
Immediate, since

{(\lambda x.\,u)}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=\lambda x.\,u
by definition.

•

t=u\,s
.
This case is not possible since there are no
v_{1},\mathtt{L}_{1}

such that
{(t\,u)}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v_{1}\mathtt{L}_{1}
.

•

t=u[x\backslash s]
.
We analyse two cases, depending on the form of

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}
.

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v_{a}\mathtt{L}_{a}
and

x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(x\mapsto v_{a})}[x\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{a}
.
Thus by hypothesis
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(x\mapsto v_{a})}\in\mathsf{Val}
.
By the
i.h.
(1) on
s
, there exist
v_{b}
and
\mathtt{L}_{b}

such that
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v_{b}\mathtt{L}_{b}
.
We are then in the case where

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(x\mapsto v_{b})}[x\backslash v_{b}]\mathtt{L}_{b}
.
We can apply the
i.h.
(1) on
u
, yielding

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(x\mapsto v_{b})}\in\mathsf{Val}
.
Therefore,

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val}
.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}]
.
Thus by hypothesis
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\in\mathsf{Val}
.
If
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then we are in the case
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.
If
\neg({s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\in\mathsf{Val})
, then the

i.h.
(1) on
s
states
\neg({s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val})
,
and so we are also in the case
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.
Hence, we can apply the
i.h.
(1) on
u
, yielding

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val}
.
Therefore,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val}
.

2.

By induction on the derivation of the judgement
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
showing only the left-to-right implication since the other is similar.

•

s-var
.
Then,
t=y\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, with
y\in\mathcal{S}
.
By hypothesis

{y}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{1}
and

{y}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={y}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}
.
Hence we conclude
{y}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v\mathtt{L}_{2}

with
\mathtt{L}_{2}:=\mathtt{L}_{1}
.

•

s-app
.
Then,
t=u\,s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, which is
derived from
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
This case is impossible since there are no
v
,
\mathtt{L}_{1}
such
that
{(u\,s)}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{1}
.

•

s-sub
1
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathcal{S}}{t=u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{s-sub${}_{1}$}}

By hypothesis,
{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{1}
.
We analyse two cases depending on the form of

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}
:

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v_{a}\mathtt{L}_{a}
and

y\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{a}
.
Thus, by hypothesis there exists
\mathtt{L}_{c}
such that

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}=v\mathtt{L}_{c}
,
where
\mathtt{L}_{1}=\mathtt{L}_{c}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{a}
.
By the
i.h.
(1) on
s
, there exist
v_{b}
,
\mathtt{L}_{b}

such that
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v_{b}\mathtt{L}_{b}
.
Hence, we are in the case

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{b})}[y\backslash v_{b}]\mathtt{L}_{b}
.
We are still in the case

{x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}={x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{b})}

for all
x\in\mathcal{S}
, since
y\notin\mathcal{S}
.
We can then apply the
i.h.
(2) on
u
, yielding
\mathtt{L}_{d}

such that
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{b})}=v\mathtt{L}_{d}
.
Therefore,

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v\mathtt{L}_{2}
,
where
\mathtt{L}_{2}=\mathtt{L}_{d}[y\backslash v_{b}]\mathtt{L}_{b}
.

–

Otherwise,

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}]
.
By hypothesis, there exists
\mathtt{L}_{c}
such that

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{c}
, where

\mathtt{L}_{1}=\mathtt{L}_{c}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}]
.
If
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.
If
\neg({s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\in\mathsf{Val})
, then the

i.h.
(1) on
s
states
\neg({s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val})
,
and so we are also in the case

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.
Hence, we can apply the
i.h.
(2) on
u
, yielding
\mathtt{L}_{d}

such that
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v\mathtt{L}_{d}
.
Therefore,

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v\mathtt{L}_{2}
,
where
\mathtt{L}_{2}=\mathtt{L}_{d}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.

•

s-sub
2
.
Then

\frac{u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{y\}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{t=u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{s-sub${}_{2}$}}

By hypothesis, there exists
\mathtt{L}_{1}
such that

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{1}
.
Depending on the form of

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}
, we have two
cases to analyse:

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v_{a}\mathtt{L}_{a}
and

x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then
v\mathtt{L}_{1}={u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{a}
.
Thus by hypothesis it must be the case that

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}=v\mathtt{L}_{c}
,
with
\mathtt{L}_{1}=\mathtt{L}_{c}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{a}
, for some
\mathtt{L}_{c}
.
By the
i.h.
(2) on
s
, there exists
\mathtt{L}_{b}
such that

{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v_{a}\mathtt{L}_{b}
.
Moreover,
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, so

{u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{b}
.
On the other hand,

{x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}={x}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}
for all

x\in\mathcal{S}\cup\{y\}
.
We then apply the
i.h.
(2) on
u
, yielding
\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}

such that

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}=v\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}
.
Hence, we conclude

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}\cup(y\mapsto v_{a})}\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{b}
,
where
\mathtt{L}_{2}=\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}[y\backslash v_{a}]\mathtt{L}_{b}
.

–

Otherwise,
v\mathtt{L}_{1}={u[y\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}]
.
Thus, by hypothesis it must be the case that

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}=v\mathtt{L}_{c}
, with

\mathtt{L}_{1}=\mathtt{L}_{c}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}]
,
for some
\mathtt{L}_{c}
.
If
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then we are in the case where

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.
If
\neg({s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{1}}\in\mathsf{Val})
, then the

i.h.
(1) on
s
states

\neg({s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\in\mathsf{Val})
, and so we are also
in the case
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.
We then apply the
i.h.
(2) on
u
, yielding
\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}

such that
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}=v\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}
.
Hence, we conclude

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
,
where
\mathtt{L}_{2}=\mathtt{L}_{c^{\prime}}[y\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma_{2}}]
.

∎

Definition C.22 (Compatibility).

Given a value assigment
\sigma
, we say
\sigma
is

compatible
with the sets of variables
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{S}
,
written
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
if the following three conditions hold:

1.

The value assignment must affect all variables in
\mathcal{A}
and some
of the variables in
\mathcal{S}

(
i.e.
,
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
).

2.

Variables in
\mathcal{A}
must be mapped to abstractions
(
i.e.
,
\sigma(x)\in\mathsf{Abs}
must hold for every
x\in\mathcal{A}
).

3.

Variables in
\mathcal{S}
affected by
\sigma
must be mapped to variables
(
i.e.
,
\sigma(x)
must be a variable for every

x\in\mathcal{S}\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
).

Lemma C.23.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{S}
be sets of variables,
t
be any term, and

\sigma
be a value assignment such that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
and

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
hold.
Then:

1.

If
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
.

2.

If
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
, then

v
is a variable.

We prove each item independently.

1.

By induction on the derivation of
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

h-var
.
Then,
t=x\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
with
x\in\mathcal{A}
.
Given
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, then
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=\sigma(x)

and
\sigma(x)\in\mathsf{Abs}
.

•

h-lam
.
Then,
t=\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
We are done, since
{(\lambda x.\,u)}^{\downarrow\sigma}=\lambda x.\,u
,
and
\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{Abs}
holds.

•

h-sub
1
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
which is
derived from
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
x\notin\mathcal{A}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
holds, then it
implies in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
.
We proceed by showing that

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
holds:

a.

Since
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}

by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, then

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
.

b.

Let
y\in\mathcal{A}
.
The premise
x\notin\mathcal{A}
implies
x\neq y
.
Thus,
\sigma(y)\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

c.

Let
y\in(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
.
Since
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
by
\alpha
-conversion, then

y\neq x
.
Thus,
\sigma(y)
is a variable by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

We can now apply the
i.h.
(1) on
u
, yielding

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
. We analyse two cases:

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
and
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
,
then
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
.
Since
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the
i.h.
(1) on
u
, then

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}\in\mathsf{Abs}
by

Remark

C.20
.
Hence, we conclude with

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Abs}
.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
Since
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the
i.h.
(1) on
u
, then

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]\in\mathsf{Abs}
.

•

h-sub
2
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
which is
derived from
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
,

x\notin\mathcal{A}
, and
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
holds, then it
implies both
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
and

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.

Applying the
i.h.
(1) on
s
, we yield
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
.
In particular,
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
.
We now prove that

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v),\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S})

holds:

a.

\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))\subseteq(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\cup\mathcal{S}

by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})

b.

Let
y\in(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})
.
If
y=x
, then

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))(x)=v
, and
v\in\mathsf{Abs}

by the
i.h.
(1) on
s
.
If
y\neq x
, then

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))(y)=\sigma(y)
,
and
\sigma(y)\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

c.

Let
y\in(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)))
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
, then
x\notin\mathcal{S}

and thus
y\neq x
.
We conclude that
(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))(y)=\sigma(y)
is a variable by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

We analyse two cases, depending on whether
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
or not:

–

If
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
.
Then,

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the

i.h.
(1) on
u
.
Therefore,
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Abs}
.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
And since
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}
.
Similarly to the previous case, we obtain

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the

i.h.
(1) on
u
, thus having
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
, so
that we conclude with

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]\in\mathsf{Abs}
.

2.

By induction on the derivation of
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

s-var
.
Then,
t=x\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
with
x\in\mathcal{S}
.
We analyse two cases depending on whether
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
or not:

–

If
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, then
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=\sigma(x)
.
Since
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, then
\sigma(x)
is a variable.

–

Otherwise,
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=x
, so we are done.

•

s-app
.
Then,
t=u\,s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
This case is not possible, as
{(u\,s)}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}\,{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is not of the form
v\mathtt{L}
.

•

s-sub
1
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
which is
derived from
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and
x\notin\mathcal{S}
.
We analyse two cases:

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v_{s}\mathtt{L}_{s}
and

x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})}[x\backslash v_{s}]\mathtt{L}_{s}
.
Since
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
has the form
v\mathtt{L}
, the same holds for

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})}
.
That is,
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})}=v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
.

Since
x\notin\mathcal{S}
by hypothesis, we have

{y}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})}={y}^{\downarrow\sigma}
for all
y\in\mathcal{S}
.
We can then apply
Lemma

C.21
(2), yielding

\mathtt{L}_{1}
such that
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}_{1}
.
Applying the
i.h.
(2) on
u
, we conclude that
v
is a
variable.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
Since
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is of the form
v\mathtt{L}
, the same holds
for
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
That is,
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
.
Following the same reasoning from case
h-sub
1
in the
previous item,
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
holds.
Moreover,
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}
by

Remark

4.1
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies in
particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
, we can then
apply the
i.h.
(2) on
u
, yielding that
v
is a variable.

•

s-sub
2
.
Then,
t=u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
which is
derived from
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}
,

x\notin\mathcal{S}
, and
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
holds, then it
implies in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
.
We analyse two cases, depending on the form

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}
has.

–

If
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v_{s}\mathtt{L}_{s}
and

x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})}[x\backslash v_{s}]\mathtt{L}_{s}
.
Moreover, since
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
, then

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})}
is
of the form
v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
holds, then it
implies in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
Given that
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and

{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v_{s}\mathtt{L}_{s}
, then

v_{s}
is a variable by the
i.h.
(2) on
s
.
We now prove that

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s}),\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})

holds.

a.

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s}))\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})

by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})

b.

Let
y\in\mathcal{A}
.
Then,
y\neq x
since

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
.
Thus,
(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s}))(y)=\sigma(y)
,
and
\sigma(y)\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

c.

Let
y\in((\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s})))
.
If
y=x
, then

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v_{s}))(x)=v_{s}

which is a variable by the
i.h.
(2) on
s
.
If
y\neq x
, then

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))(y)=\sigma(y)
,
which is a variable by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

We can then apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding that
v
is
a variable.

–

Otherwise,
{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
Moreover, since
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
, then

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is of the form
v\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
holds, then it
implies in particular
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
Given that
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
and

{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v_{s}\mathtt{L}_{s}
, then

v_{s}
is a variable by the
i.h.
(2) on
s
.
We now prove that

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})

holds.

a.

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})

by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})

b.

Let
y\in\mathcal{A}
.
Then,
y\neq x
since

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},u)}
.
Thus
\sigma(y)\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

c.

Let
y\in((\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma))
.
Then,
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
by
\alpha
-conversion, so

y\neq x
.
Thus
\sigma(y)
is a variable by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

We can then apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding that
v
is
a variable.

Lemma C.24.

Let
t[x\backslash u]
be any closure,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{S}
be sets
of variables, and
\sigma
be any value assignment such that

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t[x\backslash u])}
and
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
hold.
Then:

1.

If
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
, then

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v),\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S})
.

2.

If
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
, where

\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)
if
x\in\mathsf{rv}(t)

and
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is of the form
v\mathtt{L}
for some
v

and
\mathtt{L}
, or
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
otherwise.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t[x\backslash u])}
holds, then

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},t)}
,

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},t)}
, and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
.
We prove each item independently.

1.

We show that

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v),\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S})

holds by checking the conditions:

a.

Since
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
by
the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, then

\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\cup\{x\}\subseteq(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\cup\mathcal{S}
.

b.

Let
y\in\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}
.
If
y\neq x
, then

(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))(y)=\sigma(y)
,
and
\sigma(y)\in\mathsf{Abs}
holds by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.
Otherwise,
y=x
and
v\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Abs}
by

Lemma

C.23
(1) and thus
v\in\mathsf{Abs}
holds.

c.

Let
y\in\mathcal{S}\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v))
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},t)}
, then
x\notin\mathcal{S}

so
x\neq y
and thus the property holds by the
hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

2.

We show that
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
holds by
checking the conditions:

a.

Since
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
by
the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, then

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})
.

b.

Let
y\in\mathcal{A}
.
Since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},t)}
implies
x\notin\mathcal{A}
,
then
x\neq y
so
\sigma^{\prime}(y)=\sigma(y)
and
thus
\sigma^{\prime}(y)\in\mathsf{Abs}
by the hypothesis
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.

c.

Let
y\in(\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\})\cap\mathsf{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})
.
If
y\neq x
, then
\sigma^{\prime}(y)=\sigma(y)

and thus
\sigma^{\prime}(y)
is a variable by the hypothesis

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.
If
y=x
, there are two subcases, depending on whether

x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
.
If
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)
,
so we have that
\sigma^{\prime}(x)=v
, and we are in the case

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
, where
v
is a variable by

Lemma

C.23
(2).
If
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
, so

\sigma^{\prime}(x)=\sigma(x)
.
Note that we can assume
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
by

\alpha
-conversion, so
\sigma(x)=x
.

∎

Proposition C.25.

Let
t
be a term,
\sigma
a value assignment, and
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}

sets of variables.
Suppose
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
and
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
hold.

1.

If
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
,
then
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),\mu}
.

2.

If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
,
then either there exists a term
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\mathsf{db}}t^{\prime\prime}
, or

{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
and
\mu=@
.

Proof.

We prove each item independently.

1.

By induction on the derivation of the judgement

t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
.
The interesting cases are
NF-var
∙
,
NF-esA
∙
, and

NF-esS
∙
.

•

NF-var
∙
.
Then

\frac{x\in\mathcal{A}\Rightarrow\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}}{x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}

Where
t=x
. Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},x)}
implies

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{S}
and

\mathsf{fv}(x)=\{x\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
.
Thus, either
x\in\mathcal{A}
or
x\in\mathcal{S}
.
We analyse both cases:

–

If
x\in\mathcal{A}
, then in particular
\mu={\text{$\not$\!@}}
.
Since
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
holds,
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)

and
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{Abs}
, and moreover
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}
is of the
form
\lambda x_{1}.\,t_{1}
by definition of
\sigma(x)
.
Applying rule
NF-lam
∘
, we yield

{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.

–

If
x\in\mathcal{S}
, then
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
or not by
definition of
\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
.
If
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, then
x\in\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathcal{S}
,
and therefore
{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=y
.
By idempotency of value assignments,

y\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
.
So we yield
y\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),\mu}
by rule

NF-var
∘
.
Otherwise,
x\notin\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, thus

{x}^{\downarrow\sigma}=x\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),\mu}
by rule

NF-var
∘
.

•

NF-esA
∙
.
Then

\frac{(1)\ u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pt(2)\ s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pt(3)\ s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}}{t=u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esA${}^{\bullet}$}}

Since (3), then
s\in\mathsf{Val}
by
Remark

4.1
, and
thus (4)
{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}
by
Remark

C.19
,
for some
v
and
\mathtt{L}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}
implies both

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
and

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
Given (3) and (4), the first item of

Lemma

C.24
gives

\mathsf{compatible}(\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v),\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S})
.
Since (2), we can then apply the
i.h.
on
s
, yielding

{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}=v\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.

Since (1), we can then apply the
i.h.
on
u
, yielding
(5)
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\cup\{x\},\mu}
.
We consider two cases depending on whether
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)

or not:

–

If
x\in\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}
.
We obtain
(6)
v\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)^{\mathtt{L}},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
and

\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{CtxNF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)}
by
Lemma

C.2
.
We assume

\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{L})\mathrel{\#}\mathsf{fv}({u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)})
by

\alpha
-conversion, so

\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{L})\mathrel{\#}\mathsf{rv}({u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)})
.
Hence we can apply
Lemma

A.1
to (5), yielding
(7)
{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)^{\mathtt{L}}\cup\{x\},\mu}
.
Applying rule
NF-esVal
∘
with (7) and (6) as premises, we get

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)^{\mathtt{L}},\mu}
.
We conclude
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),\mu}
by
Lemma

C.2
.

–

If
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u[x\backslash s]}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]
.
Given
x\notin\mathsf{rv}(u)
, then

{u}^{\downarrow\sigma\cup(x\mapsto v)}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}
.
We conclude
{t}^{\downarrow\sigma}={u}^{\downarrow\sigma}[x\backslash{s}^{\downarrow\sigma}]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),\mu}
by rule
NF-esVal
∘
.

∙
.
Then

\frac{(1)\ u\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\},\mu}\hskip 8.5359pt(2)\ s\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}\hskip 8.5359pt(3)\ s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}}\textup{{NF-esS${}^{\bullet}$}}

1

Corollary C.26.

Let
t
be a term.
Then,
t\in\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\mathsf{fv}(t),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}

if and only if
{t}^{\downarrow}\in\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.

Proof.

The ‘only if’ direction is an immediate consequence of

Proposition

C.25
(
1
).

For the ‘if’ direction, it suffices to show the contrapositive,
namely that
t\notin\mathsf{NF}^{\bullet}_{\varnothing,\mathsf{fv}(t),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}

implies
{t}^{\downarrow}\notin\mathsf{NF}^{\circ}_{\varnothing,{\text{$\not$\!@}}}
.
By
Corollary

B.11
, this
implication is equivalent to showing that if

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\varnothing,\mathsf{fv}(t),{\text{$\not$\!@}}}t^{\prime}
, then there exist a
step kind
\rho^{\prime}
and a term
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

{t}^{\downarrow}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\circ$}}_{\rho^{\prime}}t^{\prime\prime}
.
This is a consequence of
Proposition

C.25

(
2
), so we are done.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of Section
6
“
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
is Invariant”

In this section, we develop the technical details that are necessary
to show that our inductive characterisation of useful evaluation is
invariant.
Recall that we proceed in two stages, following the technique used
in
[
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
]
: on one hand we prove
a high-level implementation theorem (
Theorem

6.3
).
On the other hand, we show a low-level implementation theorem (
Theorem

6.4
).
This second stage requires more development than the first one, given
that we need to relate our
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}
strategy with the GLAMoUr abstract
machine introduced in
[
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
]
.

D.1.
Low-Level Implementation

Recall that a term is
pure
if it contains no ESs.
We write
t\in\mathsf{Pure}
to denote that
t
is pure.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame and
\mathcal{S}
a structure frame.
The set of
stable terms
under
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
, written

\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
, is inductively defined as follows:

\frac{}{x\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stable-var}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\in\mathsf{Pure}}{\lambda x.\,t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stable-abs}}

\frac{t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}{t\,u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stable-app}}

\frac{t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stable-es-habs}}

\frac{t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptu\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}}{t[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stable-es-struct}}

Remark D.1.

Let
t
be a term,
\mathcal{A}
an abstraction frame, and
\mathcal{S}
a
structure frame.
If
t\in\mathsf{Pure}
, then
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

Remark D.2.

Let
t
be a term,
\mathcal{A}
an abstraction frame, and
\mathcal{S}
a
structure frame.
Then:

1.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an abstraction frame such that
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\#}\mathsf{fv}(t)
,
and
\mathcal{T}
be a structure frame such that
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\#}\mathsf{fv}(t)

and
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{T}
.
If
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
, then
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be an abstraction frame such that
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{B}

and
\mathcal{T}
be a structure frame such that
\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{T}
,
and moreover,
\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{T}
.
If
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
, then
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame and
\mathcal{S}
a structure frame.
The set of
stable substitution contexts
under
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S})
,
written
\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
, is inductively defined as follows:

\frac{}{\Diamond\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stableCtx-empty}}

\frac{\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}}{\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stableCtx-habs}}

\frac{\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptt\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359ptx\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}}{\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}}\textup{{stableCtx-struct}}

Lemma D.3.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame,
\mathcal{S}
be a structure frame,

t
be a term, and
\mathtt{L}
be a substitution context such that

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t\mathtt{L})}
holds.
Then,
t\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
if and only if

t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
and

\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We prove both sides by induction on the length of
\mathtt{L}
.

1\Rightarrow 2)

•

\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
.
On the one hand,
\mathcal{A}^{\Diamond}=\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{S}^{\Diamond}=\mathcal{S}
hold by definition,
therefore we conclude
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
by hypothesis.
Lastly, we conclude
\Diamond\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
applying rule

stableCtx-empty
.

•

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]
.
We can derive
t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]
either by rule
stable-es-habs

or rule
stable-es-struct
. Both cases are analogous, so we will only show
case
stable-es-habs
, where we have

t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
and
(1)
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
, with (2)
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}

and (3)
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
.
We can apply
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
, yielding

t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}}

and (4)
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
.
Note that
(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}\cup\{x\}=\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}
,
and since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u])}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
, then we have
u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}

by
Lemma

B.2
.
Hence by definition

\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}
.
Therefore

t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}}
.
On the other hand, we can apply rule
stableCtx-habs

with (1), (2), (3) and (4) as premises, yielding

\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

2\Rightarrow 1)

•

\mathtt{L}=\Diamond
.
Then
\mathcal{A}^{\Diamond}=\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{S}^{\Diamond}=\mathcal{S}
hold by definition,
therefore we conclude
t\Diamond=t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
by hypothesis.

•

\mathtt{L}=\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]
.
We have two cases, depending on whether

u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
or
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
Both cases are analogous, so we will only show case
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Given
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u])}
then

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},t\mathtt{L}^{\prime})}

and
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
hold, and the last statement
implies
u\notin\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
by
Lemma

B.2
.
Hence by definition

\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}=\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}\cup\{x\}=(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}
,
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}
.
Therefore (1)
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}^{\prime}}}
.
On the other hand,
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]
can only be derived by rule

stableCtx-habs
, with (2)
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
,
(3)
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
, (4)
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
and (5)
x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
.
We can apply
i.h.
on
\mathtt{L}^{\prime}
with (1) and (2) as hypothesis, yielding
(6)
t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
.
We conclude
t\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[x\backslash u]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

by applying rule
stable-es-habs
, with (3), (4), (5) and (6) as hypothesis.

∎

Lemma D.4.

Let
t
be a term,
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
be two abstraction frames,
and
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{T}
be two structure frames such that

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{T}
and

\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{T}
, and suppose that

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},t)}
holds.
If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}

with
v\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
and

t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
, then

t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of

t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.

1.

sub
∙
.
Then

t=x\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}v=t^{\prime}
,
with
\mu=@
.
Since
v\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
by hypothesis,
then
v\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}

by
Remark

D.2
.

2.

appL
∙
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 24.06294pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},@}u^{\prime}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=142.43883pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{appL${}^{\bullet}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\,s=t^{\prime}$}}}}\end{array}

and
u\,s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
can only be
derived from rule
stable-app
, so that

u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
and

s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
hold.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u\,s)}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
, therefore
we can apply
i.h.
on
u
, yielding

u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.
By
Remark

D.2
, we have

s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.
We conclude

u^{\prime}\,s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}

by applying rule
stable-app
.

3.

appR
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

4.

esR
∙
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 41.65324pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s^{\prime}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=179.62862pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{esR${}^{\bullet}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t=u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u[y\backslash s^{\prime}]=t^{\prime}$}}}}\end{array}

By
\alpha
-conversion we may assume

y\notin\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{T}
.
We can derive

u[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}

either by rule
stable-es-habs
or by rule
stable-es-struct
.
Both cases are analogous, so we only show case
stable-es-habs
.
Hence
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S}}
,

s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
,

s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
,
and
y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{S}
,
so that (1)
y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
.
Given that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u[y\backslash s])}

implies
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},s)}
,
we can apply
i.h.
on
s
, yielding
(2)
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.
Moreover,
(3)
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}

by
Remark

D.2
.
We have (4)
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by
Lemma

B.14
.
We apply rule
stable-es-habs
with (1), (2), (3) and (4) as premises, yielding

u[y\backslash s^{\prime}]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.

5.

esLA
∙
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})\cup\mathcal{S}\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=325.91975pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{esLA${}^{\bullet}$}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 73.14557pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t=u[y\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[y\backslash s]=t^{\prime}$}}}}\end{array}

Note that the judgement
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}

can be derived only by rule
stable-es-habs
.
Then
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S}}
and

s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
.
By
\alpha
-conversion, we may assume that
y\notin\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{T}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u[y\backslash s])}

implies
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\},\mathcal{S},u)}
, and
note that
v\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{y\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}

by hypothesis and
Remark

4.1
.
We can apply
i.h.
on
u
, yielding
(1)
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\{y\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.
By
Remark

D.2
we have

s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}
.
And we have
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}}

by
Remark

4.1
, and

y\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}
.
We conclude

u^{\prime}[y\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}\cup\mathcal{B},\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{T}}

by applying rule
stable-es-habs
.

6.

esLS
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

∎

Lemma D.5.

Let
t
be a term,
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame, and
\mathcal{S}
be
a structure frame such that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
holds.
If
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
with

\rho\in\{\mathsf{db},\mathsf{lsv}\}
and
t\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
,
then
t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
t\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t^{\prime}
.

•

db
∙
.
Then

t=(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}
,
where
\rho=\mathsf{db}
.
The hypothesis
(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

is derived from:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}\hskip 8.5359pts\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=142.1912pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{stable-app}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 26.29582pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}$}}}}\end{array}

Moreover,
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s)}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L})}
and

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
.
We then have

\lambda x.\,u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}

and (1)
\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

by
Lemma

D.3
,
and this judgement can only be derived from rule
stable-abs
,
hence
u\in\mathsf{Pure}
.
By
Remark

D.1
,

u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
,
and we have to analyze whether
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}}

or
s\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.
Since both cases are analogous, we will proceed with the first one.
Since
\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{L})\mathrel{\#}\mathsf{fv}(s)
by
\alpha
-conversion,
then
s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}

holds by
Remark

D.2
,
and applying rule
stable-es-habs
we have that
(2)
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.
Given that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},(\lambda x.\,u)\mathtt{L}\,s)}
implies

\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L})}
,
we can apply
Lemma

D.3
with (1) and (2),
to obtain
u[x\backslash s]\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

•

lsv
∙
.
Then

t=u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{lsv},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}=t^{\prime}
, where
\rho=\mathsf{lsv}
and it is
derived from

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\mathsf{sub}_{(x,v)},\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}
,

x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
, and
(1)
v\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
The hypothesis
u[x\backslash v\mathtt{L}]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

is derived from rule
stable-es-habs
by (1),
so (2)
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
and

v\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
By
Lemma

D.3
, we have
(3)
v\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}

and (4)
\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{StableCtx}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
On the other hand, since
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}

then
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
holds
and we can apply
Lemma

D.4
with (2) and (3) as hypothesis,
yielding
(5)
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\})^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}=\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.
Moreover (6)
v\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}}
by
Lemma

B.19
.
we may assume
x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{L})
by
\alpha
-conversion,
hence we can apply rule
stable-es-habs
with (3), (5), (6) and

x\notin\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}}\cup\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}
as premises, thus
having (7)
u^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathtt{L}},\mathcal{S}^{\mathtt{L}}}
.
We conclude
u^{\prime}[x\backslash v]\mathtt{L}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

by applying
Lemma

D.3
with
(4) and (7) as hypothesis.

•

appL
∙
.
Then

t=u\,s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}\,s=t^{\prime}
,
derived from

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},@}u^{\prime}
.
The hypothesis
u\,s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

can only be derived from rule
stable-app
,
so (1)
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
and
s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
Given that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u\,s)}

implies
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u)}
, we can apply
i.h.
on
u
,
yielding (2)
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
By rule
stable-app
with (1) and (2) as premises,
we conclude
u^{\prime}\,s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

•

appR
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

•

esR
∙
.
Then

t=u[x\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u[x\backslash s^{\prime}]=t^{\prime}
,
derived from

s\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},{\text{$\not$\!@}}}s^{\prime}
.
The hypothesis
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}

can be derived either from rule
stable-es-habs
or rule
stable-es-struct
,
so
s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
Given that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}

implies
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},s)}
, we can apply
i.h.
on
s
,
yielding
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
We can apply either rule
stable-es-habs
or
stable-es-struct

to conclude
u[x\backslash s^{\prime}]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

•

esLA
∙
.
Then

t=u[x\backslash s]\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]=t^{\prime}
,
derived from

u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},\mu}u^{\prime}
,
(1)
s\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,

x\notin\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{S}
, and
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(\rho)
.
The hypothesis
u[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
can only
be derived from rule
stable-es-habs
,
so
u\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}

and (2)
s\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.
Given that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},u[x\backslash s])}

implies
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S},u)}
, we can apply
i.h.
on
u
,
yielding (3)
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}}
.
By rule
stable-es-habs
with (1), (2) and (3) as premises, we conclude

u^{\prime}[x\backslash s]\in\mathsf{Stable}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S}}
.

•

esLS
∙
.
Analogous to the previous case.

∎

We now restrict the reduction rule
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

to stable terms.
The
stable reduction
relation, written
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
,
is defined by the same reduction rules as the relation
\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\footnotesize$\bullet$}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

(see
Section

4
), but rule
db
∙
is replaced by

\frac{u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}}{(\lambda x.\,t)\mathtt{L}\,u\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t[x\backslash u]\mathtt{L}}\textup{{db-stable${}^{\bullet}$}}

The relation of
structural equivalence
defined in

Section

6
can be defined alternatively as:

\frac{x\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(u)}{t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]\equiv t[y\backslash s][x\backslash u]}\textup{{es-comm}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{y\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)}{t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]\equiv t[x\backslash u[y\backslash s]]}\textup{{es-assoc}}

\frac{x\notin\mathsf{fv}(u)}{(t\,u)[x\backslash s]\equiv t[x\backslash s]\,u}\textup{{es-l-dist}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)}{(t\,u)[x\backslash s]\equiv t\,u[x\backslash s]}\textup{{es-r-dist}}

\frac{t\equiv t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptu\equiv u^{\prime}}{t\,u\equiv t^{\prime}\,u^{\prime}}\textup{{cong-app}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\equiv t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptu\equiv u^{\prime}}{t[x\backslash u]\equiv t^{\prime}[x\backslash u^{\prime}]}\textup{{cong-es}}

\frac{}{t\equiv t}\textup{{refl}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\equiv u}{u\equiv t}\textup{{sym}}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{t\equiv u\hskip 8.5359ptu\equiv s}{t\equiv s}\textup{{trans}}

Remark D.6.

Let
t
be a term,
v
be a value, and
\mathtt{L}
be a list of ESs.
If
v\mathtt{L}\equiv t
then
t
is of the form
v^{\prime}\mathtt{L^{\prime}}
,
and the proof of equivalence necessarily uses rule
refl
.

Remark D.7 (Strengthening of Abstraction and Value Frames).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame and
\mathcal{S}
be a structure set.
Let
t
be a term such that
x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)
.
Then:

1.

If
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}\cup\{x\}}
then
t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

If
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}\cup\{x\}}
then
t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Lemma D.8 (Hereditary Abstractions and Structures are Closed by Structural Equivalence).

Let
t
be a term,
\mathcal{A}
be an abstraction frame, and
\mathcal{S}
be
a structure frame such that
\mathsf{inv}{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},t)}
holds.
If
t\equiv u
, then:

1.

t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
if and only if
u\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

t\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
if and only if
u\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

Each item is proved
by induction on the derivation of
t\equiv u
.
∎

See
6.2

Proof.

We start by introducing an auxiliary equivalence relation
\equiv_{c}

on terms, recursively defined by:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 21.6299pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty x\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)\hskip 8.5359pty\notin\mathsf{fv}(u)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=124.62907pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-comm}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]\equiv_{c}t[y\backslash s][x\backslash u]$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 45.01707pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty y\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=124.62907pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-assoc}}(1)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]\equiv_{c}t[x\backslash u[y\backslash s]]$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 45.01707pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty y\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=124.62907pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-assoc}}(2)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t[x\backslash u[y\backslash s]]\equiv_{c}t[x\backslash u][y\backslash s]$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.02751pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty x\notin\mathsf{fv}(u)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=95.21709pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-l-dist}}(1)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(t\,u)[x\backslash s]\equiv_{c}t[x\backslash s]\,u$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 29.02751pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty x\notin\mathsf{fv}(u)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=95.21709pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-l-dist}}(2)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t[x\backslash s]\,u\equiv_{c}(t\,u)[x\backslash s]$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.08424pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=95.21709pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-r-dist}}(1)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(t\,u)[x\backslash s]\equiv_{c}t\,u[x\backslash s]$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.08424pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty x\notin\mathsf{fv}(t)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=95.21709pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{es-r-dist}}(2)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t\,u[x\backslash s]\equiv_{c}(t\,u)[x\backslash s]$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\equiv_{c}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptu\equiv_{c}u^{\prime}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=62.07692pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{cong-app}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 9.91595pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t\,u\equiv_{c}t^{\prime}\,u^{\prime}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.13261pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\equiv_{c}t^{\prime}\hskip 8.5359ptu\equiv_{c}u^{\prime}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=80.34215pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textup{{{{cong-es}}}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t[x\backslash u]\equiv_{c}t^{\prime}[x\backslash u^{\prime}]$}}}}\end{array}

Note in particular that

\equiv
is the reflexive-transitive closure of
\equiv_{c}
.
We divide the proof into two parts:

1.

We show that if
t_{0}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t_{1}
and

t_{0}\equiv_{c}u_{0}
, then there exists
u_{1}
such that

u_{0}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}u_{1}
and

t_{1}\equiv u_{1}
.

2.

Given that
\equiv
is the reflexive-transitive closure of
\equiv_{c}
,
we show the same result but for the
\equiv
relation by resorting to

Item

1
.

Item

2
is proved by induction on the reflexive and transitive
closure of
\equiv_{c}
and is immediate.
The proof of
Item

1
is by induction on the derivation of

t_{0}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\rho,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}t_{1}
and case analysis.

•

db-stable
∙
.
Then

t_{0}=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,r\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-2.2604pt}{{\footnotesize$\blacktriangle$}}}_{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}s[x\backslash r]\mathtt{L}=t_{1}
,
with
r\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}\cup\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
We also have
t_{0}=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,r\equiv_{c}u_{0}
.
We analyze the different cases according to which rule was used to derive the equivalence.
Note that cases

,

and

.

∙
and

∙
.

by rule

.

∙

\textstyle{t_{0}=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}\,r^{\prime}[y\backslash p]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\footnotesize\blacktriangle}

c.

cong-app
.
Then

(1)

–

es-comm
.
Then

, and

(\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu)
-reduces to

∙

Lemma

given that

\textstyle{t_{0}=(\lambda x.\,s)\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[y_{1}\backslash q_{1}][y_{2}\backslash q_{2}]\,r\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}

\textstyle{\equiv_{c}}

\scriptstyle{\footnotesize\blacktriangle}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{db},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mu}
\textstyle{s[x\backslash r^{\prime}]\mathtt{L}^{\prime}[y_{1}\backslash q_{1}[y_{2}\backslash q_{2}]]=u_{1}}

∙

The remaining cases are treated in a similar way.

∎

D.1.1.
Simulation of GLAMOUr steps in
\textup{{ucbv}}^{\bullet}

For completeness, we recall the syntax and relevant details of the
GLAMoUr abstract machine.

Definition D.9 (Syntax of the GLAMoUr).

The set of
states

(s,s^{\prime},\ldots)
,

dumps

(D,D^{\prime},\ldots)
,

stacks

(\pi,\pi^{\prime},\ldots)
,

stack items

(\phi,\psi,\ldots)
, and

global environments

(E,E^{\prime},\ldots)

are given by the following grammars:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}s&\mathrel{::=}&(D,\mathbf{t},\pi,E)\\
D&\mathrel{::=}&\epsilon\mid D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)\\
\pi&\mathrel{::=}&\epsilon\mid\phi^{l}:\pi&\text{where $l\in\{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{S}\}$}\\
\phi&\mathrel{::=}&\mathbf{t}\mid(\mathbf{t},\pi)\\
E&\mathrel{::=}&\epsilon\mid[x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E&\text{where $l\in\{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{S}\}$}\end{array}

where
codes

(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u},\ldots)
are
terms with no explicit substitutions (
i.e.
, pure terms),
but
they are not
considered up to
\alpha
-equivalence.
We use to decorate stack items with
labels

l\in\{\mathbb{A},\mathbb{S}\}
,
writing
\phi^{l}
rather than
\phi
.
By convention this label always indicates the shape of
\phi
,
so in particular,

l=\mathbb{A}
if and only if
\phi
is of the form
t
,
and

l=\mathbb{S}
if and only if
\phi
is of the form
(t,\pi)
.
Intuitively, these labels indicate whether a stack item unfolds to
a hereditary abstraction (
\mathbb{A}
) or a structure (
\mathbb{S}
).

Let
s=(D,\mathbf{t},\pi,E)
.
A
binding occurrence
for a variable
x
in
s
is
either the leftmost occurrence of
x
in an abstraction
\lambda x.\,\mathbf{t}

or the leftmost occurrence of
x
in an element
[x\backslash\phi]

of the environment.
We say that
s
is
well-named

if the three following conditions hold:

1.

Each variable
x
has at most one binding occurrence.
For example,

((\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,y,\epsilon),w,\epsilon,[z\backslash w])

is well-named, while

(\epsilon,\lambda x.\,x,\epsilon,[x\backslash w])
and

(\epsilon,\lambda x.\,\lambda x.\,y,\epsilon,\epsilon)

are not.

2.

If there is some binding occurrence for a variable
x

in an abstraction,then, all occurrences of
x
only
occur inside the body of this abstraction.
For example,

(\epsilon,\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x,x,\epsilon)

is not well-named.

3.

If there is some binding occurrence for a variable
x

in an environment of the form
E_{1}:[x\backslash\phi]:E_{2}
, then there are no other occurrences of
x

in
E_{2}
.
For example,

((\epsilon,\epsilon),y,y,[x\backslash y][z\backslash x])

is not well-named.

Definition D.10 (Decoding of components).

The decoding of the components of the GLAMoUr abstract machine
into the syntax of our calculus is given by the following function:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\{\!\!\{(D,\mathbf{t},\pi,E)\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}\langle\{\!\!\{D\}\!\!\}\langle\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\rangle\rangle\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{\epsilon\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&\Diamond\\
\{\!\!\{D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\!\!\{D\}\!\!\}\langle\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\Diamond\rangle\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}:\pi\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle\Diamond\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{[x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}\langle\Diamond[x\backslash\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}]\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{(\mathbf{t},\pi)^{l}\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\rangle\\
\{\!\!\{\mathbf{t}^{l}\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&t\\
\{\!\!\{\mathbf{t}\}\!\!\}&\mathrel{:=}&t\end{array}

In the two last lines,
t
denotes a term which is
\alpha
-equivalent to
\mathbf{t}
.

Definition D.11 (Transitions of the GLAMoUr abstract machine).

The transitions of the GLAMoUr abstract machine are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|ccc|c|c|ccc}D&\mathbf{t}\,\mathbf{s}&\pi&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{1}}&D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{s}&\epsilon&E\\
D&\mathbf{\lambda x.\,t}&\phi^{l}:\pi&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{m}}}&D&\mathbf{t}&\pi&[x\backslash\phi^{l}]:E\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{\lambda x.\,s}&\epsilon&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{2}}&D&\mathbf{t}&(\mathbf{\lambda x.\,s})^{\mathbb{A}}:\pi&E\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{x}&\pi^{\prime}&E&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{3}}&D&\mathbf{t}&(\mathbf{x},\pi^{\prime})^{\mathbb{S}}:\pi&E&\mathbf{x}\notin\mathtt{dom}(E)\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{x}&\pi^{\prime}&E_{1}:[x\backslash\phi^{\mathbb{S}}]:E_{2}&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{4}}&D&\mathbf{t}&(\mathbf{x},\pi^{\prime})^{\mathbb{S}}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\phi^{\mathbb{S}}]:E_{2}\\
D:(\mathbf{t},\pi)&\mathbf{x}&\epsilon&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{c}}_{5}}&D&\mathbf{t}&\mathbf{x}^{\mathbb{A}}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}\\
D&\mathbf{x}&\phi^{l}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}&\leadsto_{{\mathtt{u}}{\mathtt{e}}}&D&\mathbf{s}^{\alpha}&\phi^{l}:\pi&E_{1}:[x\backslash\mathbf{s}^{\mathbb{A}}]:E_{2}\end{array}

where
\mathbf{u}^{\alpha}
is any code
\alpha
-equivalent to

\mathbf{u}
that preserves well-naming of the machine. Note that in

[
Accattoli and Coen, 2015
]
there are two syntactically different sorts of
variables, corresponding to free and bound variables
respectively. Here we use only one sort of variables,
but this is just a matter of presentation.

We say that a state
s_{0}
is
initial
if and only if it is
of the form
s_{0}=(\epsilon,\mathbf{t},\epsilon,\epsilon)
for some
code
\mathbf{t}
.
A state
s
is
reachable
if and only if there exists an
initial state
s_{0}
such that
s_{0}\leadsto^{*}s
.

Let
s_{0}=(\epsilon,\mathbf{t}_{0},\epsilon,\epsilon)
be an initial
state in the GLAMoUr, and
E
a global environment.
We say that a stack item
\phi
is
E
-rigid

if and only if:

1.

\phi^{l}=\mathbf{t}

implies
\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}=t\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
where
\mathcal{A}=\varnothing^{\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}}
is an abstraction frame,
and
\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}
is the list of substitution contexts resulting
from the decoding of
E
.
This is equivalent to saying that if
l=\mathbb{A}
then
\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}\in\mathsf{HA}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

\phi^{l}=(\mathbf{t},\pi)

implies
\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}=\{\!\!\{\pi\}\!\!\}\langle t\rangle\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
where
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{fv}(\mathbf{t}_{0})^{\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}}
is a structure frame,
and
\{\!\!\{E\}\!\!\}
is the list of substitution contexts resulting
from the decoding of
E
.
This is equivalent to saying that if
l=\mathbb{S}
then
\{\!\!\{\phi^{l}\}\!\!\}\in\mathsf{St}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Remark D.12.

1.
